PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
_____________________________________________
INFORMATION NOTE
What this Part is about: This Part sets out the responsibility of the parties
to manage their litigation.
Once a statement of claim, defence, reply, and in more complicated
actions a counterclaim or third party claim and related pleadings have
been filed and served, each party knows what claims and defences are
being made in an action. The rules then
- require the parties to manage the litigation so that the parties know
when important stages in the action should be complete,
- require the parties to consider and participate in a dispute resolution
process, unless the Court waives this requirement,
provide a means for the parties to obtain Court assistance for
managing the litigation, including the appointment of a case
management judge, and
establish a structure for the parties to ask a judge to actively facilitate
resolution of a claim through judicial dispute resolution.
This Part also includes
- rules describing how an award for security for payment of costs can
be obtained, and
- rules for a formal offer to settle the litigation and a description of the
potential costs consequences when settlement is not pursued, to
ensure that formal offers are given careful consideration.
Lastly, this Part deals with litigation delay, the effects of the death of a
party, and discontinuing an action.
Words and phrases used in this Part that have defined meanings in
the Appendix [Definitions] include: Chief Justice; claim; costs award;
Court; court clerk; defendant; expert; file; health care professional; judge;
judgment; medical examination; order; party; plaintiff; pleading; prescribed
form; procedural order; record; remedy; rules

4-3
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.1
DIVISION 1
RESPONSIBILITIES OF PARTIES
Responsibilities of Parties to Manage Litigation
4.1 The parties are responsible for managing their dispute and for planning its
resolution in a timely and cost effective way.
How to interpret this Rule is unclear. Is it a preamble? An interpretation section? Are there
penalties for its breach? Does it affect costs? What are its limits or precise content, if so? Is it
merely aspirational? It is very unlikely that it overrules clear other Rules of Court. Rule 4.2 adds
more contents, but little more context. See R.4.2 n. , and R.4.4 n.
Party autonomy and parties’ responsibility for control of litigation was one of the objectives of
the 2010 Rules.¹
The court criticized counsel who had been ordered to ﬁle a book of agreed exhibits. Late, they
handed in large clumps of disorganized and repetitive papers. The clumps ﬁled had no table of
contents, index, or description. At trial, hundreds of pages of those documents were not referred.
Neither party got any costs.²
Delay in litigation is always of concern, both to all existing litigants, and also to others
considering whether to sue, or awaiting precedent-making decisions. Especially to those about
to join the back of the queue. It is never in the public interest to have property or business idle
and tied up in litigation. Various statutory limitation periods specify the time within which an action
must be started. Once the action is started, the Rules of Court impose a number of deadlines for
speciﬁc ensuing steps. For example, R.3.26 requires that the statement of claim be served within
one year of issuance, and R.3.31 allows 20 days to ﬁle a defence. Rule 13.5 gives the court a
power to enlarge or abridge most of the speciﬁc time limits. And delay in getting on to trial may
be a ground to end interlocutory remedies previously given.³
Part 4, Divisions 1, 2 and 6 of the
Rules of Court complement these other provisions by providing general rules governing delay in
litigation and providing for remedies for that delay.
Part 4 provides for the following things:
1. The parties to litigation can agree on the pace and timing of the litigation.⁴
2. The court may dismiss the action when delay has caused prejudice to a party.⁵
3. If three years elapse without any signiﬁcant advance in the proceeding, the action must
be dismissed.⁶
4. The court can allow an action to proceed but if there was undue delay, only on terms that
will negate any prejudice, if point #3 does not apply.⁷
The “responsibility” mentioned in this and similar Rules includes reacting positively to non-
prejudicial slips by the opposing counsel.⁸

3 Kempenaar v. Kempenaar 2008 ABQB 476.
4 See the annotation below to Rule 4.4. (1).

636, JCC 0701 00381 (Oct 27).

4-4
R.4.2 PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
Part 4 applies to most types of action¹
and most kinds of litigants.²
Even as part of an appeal, a litigant should not ask for procedural or legal advice from the
court.³
Courts continue to forgive slips or late ﬁlings, if the delay was small, and the fault of that party
was small, no harm was done, and the party tried at once to cure the ﬂaw.⁴

Footnote
2020 amendments to R.4.16, let a case management judge or case conference judge order
a private alternate dispute resolution. A private mediation of ﬁnancial issues was ordered. If the
parties could not agree on a mediator, the judge would pick one. It could be electronic. The judge
recommended that any unresolved issues after that be given an independent ﬁnancial adviser’s
advice.⁵

Related Provisions
1.2 (purpose and intention of the rules) ; 4.3 (delay) ; 10.33(2) (a) (costs of misconduct) ; 10.49

(penalty for contravening the rules) ; 14.55 (managing appeals)
What the Responsibility Includes
4.2 The responsibility of the parties to manage their dispute and to plan its
resolution requires the parties
(a) to act in a manner that furthers the purpose and intention of these
rules described in rule 1.2 [Purpose and intention of these rules],
(b) in an action categorized as a standard case, to respond in a
substantive way and within a reasonable time to any proposal for
the conduct of an action,
(c) in an action categorized as a complex case, to meet or to adjust
dates in a timely way in a complex case litigation plan,
(d) when the complexity or the nature of an action requires it, to apply to
the Court for direction, or request case management under rule 4.12
[Request for case management], and
(e) to consider and engage in one or more dispute resolution processes
described in rule 4.16(1) [Dispute resolution processes] unless the
Court waives that requirement.
Information Note
This Part does not apply to an action brought by originating application unless the parties
otherwise agree or the Court otherwise orders. See rule 3.10 [Application of Part 4 and Part
5] and rule 3.12 [Application of statement of claim rules to originating applications].
1 Including a class action: C. H. S. v. Dir. of Child Welfare 2008 ABQB 513, 452 AR 66. And a divorce suit:
Kempenaar v. Kempenaar, supra. See C. P. E. , Chapter 36, Part C.
2 See C. P. E. , Chapter 36, Part D. But rarely against infant plaintiffs: C. H. S. v. Dir. of Child Welfare, supra.
3 Shaw v. Boyda 2015 ABCA 393, Calg 1501 0291 AC (one JA Dec 11). But the case management ofﬁcer of
the Court of Appeal can give such advice.
4 Marshall v. Lee 2024 ABCA 86, Calg 2301 0178 AC (one JA Mar 13).
5 J. M. S. v. J. D. S.2020 ABQB 272, JCE 4803 175915 (Apr 20) (¶’s 64-67).

4-5
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.3(1)
Under rule 8.4 [Trial date: scheduled by court clerk] no trial date can be scheduled by the
court clerk until the parties have engaged in a dispute resolution process unless the Court
waives this requirement. The waiver can be granted under rule 4.16(2) [Dispute resolution
processes]. A waiver under rule 4.16(2) can also be given in the course of an application
under rule 8.5(1) [Trial date: scheduled by the Court], see rule 8.5(1) (a) (iii).

Related Provisions
1.2 (purpose and intention of the rules) ; 4.12 (request for case management) ; 4.16 (dispute

resolution processes)
The identiﬁcation of cases as “standard” or “complex” is new to the 2010 Rules, and represents
a variation on the litigation tracks adopted in other jurisdictions.
The parties remain primarily responsible for managing their dispute.¹
However, this
responsibility is subject to R.1.2(3), which requires the parties to act in a way that is consistent
with the intent and purpose of the Rules: that claims are to be fairly and justly resolved in a timely
and cost effective way.
A defendant’s saying that it did not at this early stage know enough facts to set up a litigation
timetable, is not obstruction. Especially as the plaintiffs said that they proposed summary judgment
and that they would proceed if they did not hear from the defendant.²
Categories of Court Action
4.3(1) For the purpose of these rules, actions are categorized as
(a) standard cases, or
(b) complex cases.
(2) In deciding whether an action should be categorized as a standard or complex
case, the parties or the Court, as the case requires, must consider the following
factors:
(a) the amount of the claim, the number and nature of the claims, and
the complexity of the action;
(b) the number of parties;
(c) the number of documents involved;
(d) the number and complexity of issues and how important they are;
(e) how long questioning under Part 5 [Disclosure of Information] is
likely to take;
(f) whether expert reports will be required and, if so, the time it will take
to exchange reports and to question experts under Part 5 [Disclosure
of Information];
(g) whether medical examinations and reports under Part 5 [Disclosure
of Information], Division 3 [Medical Examinations by Health Care
Professionals] will be required;

Footnote
1 Kent v. Martin 2013 ABQB 36, 549 AR 398.
2 Richardson Pioneer v. Hutterian Brethren Church etc.2021 ABQB 20, JCE 1603 05986 (Jan 8) (¶ 24).

4-6
R.4.3(3) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
(h) any other matter that should be considered to meet the purpose and
intention of the rules described in rule 1.2 [Purpose and intention of
these rules];
(i) whether a third party claim has been or is likely to be made.
(3) If, within 4 months after the date a statement of defence is ﬁled, the parties
do not agree on whether the action is a standard or complex case, and the Court
does not otherwise order, the action is to be categorized as a standard case.

Related Provisions
1.2 (purpose and intention of the Rules) ; Part 5 (disclosure of information) ; 3.44 (when a third

party claim may be filed); 3.1 (statement of defence)
Standard Case Obligations
4.4(1) Unless the parties otherwise agree, or the Court otherwise orders, and
subject to matters arising beyond the control of the parties, the parties to an
action categorized as a standard case must, within a reasonable time considering
the nature of the action, complete each of the following steps or stages in the
action:
(a) close of pleadings;
(b) disclosure of information under Part 5 [Disclosure of Information];
(c) at least one of the dispute resolution processes described in rule
4.16(1), unless the requirement is waived by the Court;
(d) application for a trial date.
(2) A party to an action categorized as a standard case may serve on the other
party a proposed litigation plan or a proposal for the completion or timing of any
stage or step in the action, and if no agreement is reached, any party may apply
to the Court for a procedural or other order respecting the plan or proposal.¹
Information Note
Close of pleadings (referred to in subrule (1) (a) is a time determined under rule 3.67 [Close
of pleadings]).

Related Provisions
3.67 (close of pleadings) ; 4.31 (delay) ; 4.32 (agreement to delay action) ; 8.5 (trial date:

scheduled by the Court)
Rule 4.4(1) allows the parties expressly to opt out of R.4.4.
This Rule (like the previous Rules) allows any party to serve on another party a proposal as to
the pace or timing of the action. This procedure is useful where there has already been delay in an
action, and one of the parties believes that the general provisions of the Rules are not sufﬁcient
to keep the action moving. This Rule can also serve a preventative function: it can be used before
trouble arises, in an action that is unusually complex or that involves a large number of parties, to
avoid delay arising.

Footnote
1 Quite similar to previous 1968 R.243.2’s beginning. It was new in 1994, enacted by Alta. Reg.234/94.

4-7
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.5(1)
One need not use this Rule before bringing an application to dismiss an action for delay, but
its prior use can build a case for such an application. If any party fails to agree to the proposal for
pace and timing, the proposal provides an important background to an application to dismiss, or
to an application under R.4.9, or to an application for case management.¹
Once the 2010 Rules
came in, the parties acquired an obligation either to settle or jointly move an ongoing suit from
stage to stage.
In one case, if one party thought that the matter was not progressing fast enough, it should
have proposed a litigation timetable. Only after failure to reach agreement on procedural steps
can there be an application to the court under R.4.4(2). The court should not give an order for a
litigation plan before the parties have tried to negotiate one, as that would thwart the principles
in the new Rules of joint management of litigation. The object of the Rule is not to have the court
save the proceeding at the last moment, where the plaintiff had shown no intention to proceed
with the action until there was a motion to dismiss for want of prosecution.²
A completed litigation plan is a procedural step required by R.4.4(1), so completing that step
will always materially advance the action?³
But merely moving for it will not.⁴
Complex Case Obligations
4.5(1) The parties to an action categorized as a complex case must, within 4
months after the date that the parties agree to the categorization or the Court
determines that the action is a complex case,
(a) agree on a complex case litigation plan, and
(b) unless reasons are given in the plan not to do so,
(i) establish a date by which the real issues in dispute will be
identiﬁed,
(ii) agree on a protocol for the organization and production of
records,
(iii) set a date by which disclosure of records will be completed
under rule 5.5 [When an afﬁdavit of records must be served],
(iv) set a date by which questioning under Part 5 [Disclosure of
Information] will be completed,
(v) set a date by which all experts’ reports and rebuttal and
surrebuttal expert reports will be served,
(vi) set a date by which reports of any health care professionals
will be obtained, and
(vii) agree on an estimated date to apply for a trial date.
(2) When a complex case litigation plan or an amendment to the plan is agreed
to, the plaintiff must ﬁle it and serve it on all parties.

action have been heavily criticized or forbidden.

Complex case litigation plans are new under the 2010 Rules. They have no speciﬁed format.
Settling Disputes About Complex Case Litigation Plans
4.6 If no agreement is reached on a complex case litigation plan within the period
referred to in rule 4.5(1) [Complex case obligations], or if the parties cannot
agree on an adjustment to a date in the plan, the Court may
(a) establish or amend a complex case litigation plan for the action, or
(b) make a procedural order with respect to the action generally or to
deal with particular issues or issues that may arise.

Monitoring and Adjusting Dates
4.7(1) The parties must monitor progress in their action and adjust the dates
by which a stage or step in the action is expected to be completed if a party is
added to an action or as circumstances require.
(2) On application, the Court may adjust or set dates by which a stage or a step
in the action is expected to be complete.

Related Provisions
3.74 (adding, removing or substituting parties) ; 4.31 (delay) ; 4.32 (agreement to delay

action); 6.3 (applications generally) ; 13.5 (variation of time).
Court May Categorize Actions
4.8(1) On application, the Court may direct whether an action is to be categorized
as a standard or complex case.
(2) The Court may change the categorization of an action to complex or standard
at any time.

Related Provisions
1.4 (procedural orders) ; 6.3 (applications generally)


4-9
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.9
DIVISION 2
COURT ASSISTANCE IN MANAGING LITIGATION
Orders to Facilitate Proceedings
4.9 If a party or the Court is not satisﬁed that an action is being managed in
accordance with rule 4.2 [What the responsibility includes],
(a) the party may apply for a procedural order, an order under rule 4.10
[Assistance by the Court] or any other appropriate order;
(b) the Court may make a procedural order, an order under rule 4.10
[Assistance by the Court] or any other appropriate order.

Generally on the powers of a judge to manage and supervise litigation, see the Fuerst book.¹
In the “News and Announcements” part of the Court of King's Bench’s website there is a
prominent link to “Case Management”. Clicking on it produces a number of past notices to the
profession, arranged in date order, many from the last months of 2019. A few are not really about
case management, but may be of some use to counsel or parties working on case management
topics. If desired, any of them can be downloaded. The August 31, 2020 notice is about “family
case management” (also available under the “family” link). “Amended Family Practice Note 9”
is another notice (December 18, 2016) under that same link. It is about Calgary and Red Deer
management, and all the Practice Notes are in volume 3 of this book. Probably the October 9,

Footnote
2019 Notice to the Profession remains in effect, so one cannot obtain case management directly
(unless one falls into the narrow exceptions where it is required, like civil juries). Instead, parties
must try a Case Conference ﬁrst under R.4.10, at which the presiding justice will assess if case
management is appropriate. If so, the parties can write to the Associate Chief Justice and request
it.
Though Canadian courts are reactive and not inquisitorial (as in Germany), “using this venue
is a privilege not a right”. So in lengthy acrimonious litigation where attempts to settle a formal
order had failed, the Master gave directions to move the dispute forward, effective at once and
without need for a formal order.²
The Court of Appeal was critical of delay in producing various records, but declined to upset
the decision of a case management judge who had refused to ﬁnd contempt of court (and in part
excused it). Part of the result ﬂowed from the high level of deference which is to be given to a case
management judge.³
Assistance by the Court
4.10(1) The Court may, at any time, direct the parties and any other person to
attend a conference with the Court.
1 Fuerst & Sanderson, Ont. Courtroom Procedure, Chap.2 Parts D.1, D.2 (4th ed.2016).
2 Dalton v. Draper (M) 2014 ABQB 388, [2014] AR Uned 472.
3 Demb v. Valhalla Grp.2015 ABCA 29, 593 AR 368.

4-10
R.4.10(2) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
(2) The participants in the conference may consider
(a) dispute resolution possibilities, the process for them, and how they
can be facilitated;
(b) simpliﬁcation or clariﬁcation of a claim, a pleading, a question, an
issue, an application or a proceeding;
(c) a complex case litigation plan or a modiﬁcation to the plan;
(d) case management by a judge;
(e) practice, procedural or other issues or questions and how to resolve
them;
(f) any other matter that may aid in the resolution or facilitate the
resolution of a claim, application or proceeding or otherwise meet
the purpose and intention of these rules described in rule 1.2
[Purpose and intention of these rules].
(3) If a party ﬁles an application for a conference under this rule, that party must
(a) give a reason for the conference, and
(b) ﬁle and serve on every other party notice of the application and any
material to be relied on in support of the application a reasonable
time before the date the conference is scheduled to take place.
(4) The Court may make a procedural order before, at or following the conference.¹

Related Provisions
1.4 (procedural orders) ; 4.12 (request for case management) ; 4.16 (dispute resolution

processes); 6.3 (applications generally) ; 14.55 (managing appeals) ; 14.73 (powers on
appeal)
On a judge managing discovery (production) of records, see the Dreco case.²
Where there is a class action with a case management judge, any motion to opt out of the suit
should be made before the case management judge; it would require leave of the Chief Justice or
Associate Chief Justice for any other judge to hear such a matter.³
Rule 4.21 bars a judicial dispute resolution judge from hearing anything in that suit without the
“written consent” of every party (and of the judge). Without that, the judge lacks jurisdiction to hear
even an application purportedly ﬂowing from the judicial dispute resolution or an agreement at it.⁴
A case management judge’s decisions get considerable deference on appeal, if there were no
errors in principle or law arbitrary decisions, or clear injustice.⁵
All steps leading up to whether to
give a summary decision get such deference.⁶

4-11
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.11
4-11
Ways the Court May Manage Action
4.11 The Court may manage an action in one or more of the following ways, in
which case the responsibility of the parties to manage their dispute is modiﬁed
accordingly:
(a) the Court may make a procedural order;
(b) the Court may direct a conference under rule 4.10 [Assistance by the
Court];
(c) on request under rule 4.12 [Request for case management], or on
the initiative of the Chief Justice under rule 4.13 [Appointment of
case management judge], the Chief Justice may appoint a case
management judge for the action;
(d) the Court may make an order under a rule providing for speciﬁc
direction or a remedy.

Related Provisions
14.55 (managing appeals; 14.73 (powers on appeal)

Case management is designed to keep a lawsuit running smoothly and progressing, and to
try for judicial dispute resolution, not to give the parties preferred access to a judge to hear more
and more interlocutory applications.¹
Where a judge has set timelines for trying and deciding disputed questions of costs, if one
party then tenders late further afﬁdavits of marginal weight, the court may refuse to accept them,
to avoid further delay.²
A trial judge correctly rejects a new argument made after close of evidence, which would take
the opposing party by surprise, especially if the issues had earlier been deﬁned and settled. And
if no evidence was led on other issues.³
Case management decisions are not lightly upset or
reopened. All that binds, whether or not there is any other kind of estoppel.⁴
The court criticized a judge who was not the existing case management judge for hearing
an important contested application, where there was no urgency and there had been many
indications that the court had to be cautious with the parties and their procedure.⁵
During oral discovery, if it turns out that there are records not yet produced, repeated motions
to strike pleadings for that are an overreaction; better is case management by the existing case
management judge.⁶
A party sought to have case management ended, in a high-conﬂict lawsuit. There were a
number of issues to resolve before trial not suitable for ordinary morning chambers. But any
delays so far had been because of the complexity of the matter.⁷

and 4.14. And new R.15.2 preserves all done under the old Rules.

the party complaining twice turned down).

4-12
R.4.12(1) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
Request for Case Management
4.12(1) A request for a case management order must be made in writing to the
Chief Justice and a copy of the request must be served on each of the other
parties.
(2) The request must state
(a) the reason for the request, and
(b) whether any of the other parties agrees with the request.
(3) An action commenced or continued under the Class Proceedings Act must
have a case management judge appointed for the action unless the Chief Justice
decides otherwise, and the request for a case management judge must be made
no later than the date on which the ﬁrst application in respect of the class
proceeding is made under section 2(2) of the Class Proceedings Act.

Related Provisions
8.2(5) (jury trials)

Sometimes “case management” is a term used loosely, but under the Rules, it has a speciﬁc
meaning. That is a formal written application under R.4.12 to the Chief Justice to designate a
judge to take the whole action under case management.¹
Appointment of Case Management Judge
4.13 The Chief Justice may order that an action be subject to case management
and appoint a judge as the case management judge for the action for one or
more of the following reasons:
(a) to encourage the parties to participate in a dispute resolution
process;
(b) to promote and ensure the fair and efﬁcient conduct and resolution
of the action;
(c) to keep the parties on schedule;
(d) to facilitate preparation for trial and the scheduling of a trial date.

Related Provisions
8.2(5) (jury trials)

When there was a vexatious litigant order requiring permission of a certain Justice to start
proceedings, and the Justice had a possible conﬂict, she still had the power to order that a
different judge hear the request. A case management Judge is not prevented from hearing such
a motion by being shortly before appointed to the Court of Appeal, especially as she remains an
ex officio King's Bench judge.²

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.14(1)
Accusations of bias against case management judges are fairly common, but rarely well-
founded. It is proper for such a Judge to keep continuity by still managing a case after moving to
another city, or to have the Attorney General get notice of an important issue which no one wishes
to argue one side of, or to form a preliminary opinion about an application after reading all the
written arguments and other material.¹
No bias was found in a case management judge’s comments.²
If a party disappointed with
the judge could get a fresh judge, case management would mean nothing.³
And the Rule which
calls for leave to appeal from the judge to be appealed, does not create institutional bias. A case
management judge will not be the trial judge, and so can speak ﬁrmly and frankly to counsel; and
the case management judge has a duty to keep the suit moving.⁴
It is presumed that the judge
will use known tests for giving or withholding leave, and costs appeals have an established 4-part
test, which was used here.⁵
The chief justice’s appointment of a judge to case manage one suit lets the parties acquiesce
in that judge’s managing another related suit by the same plaintiff.⁶
Authority of Case Management Judge
4.14(1) A case management judge, or if the circumstances require, any other
judge, may
(a) order that steps be taken by the parties to identify, simplify or clarify
the real issues in dispute,
(b) establish, substitute or amend a complex case litigation plan and
order the parties to comply with it,
(c) make an order to facilitate an application, proceeding, questioning
or pre-trial proceeding,
(d) make an order to promote the fair and efﬁcient resolution of the
action by trial,
(e) facilitate efforts the parties may be willing to take towards the
efﬁcient resolution of the action or any issue in the action through
negotiation or a dispute resolution process other than trial,
(f) make any procedural order that the judge considers necessary, or
(g) as a case management judge, exercise the powers that a trial
judge has by adjudicating any issues that can be decided before
commencement of the trial, including those related to
(i) the admissibility of evidence,
(ii) expert witnesses,

Related Provisions
4.5 (complex case obligations) ; 4.15 (restrictions on case management; 13.1 (any judge can

act)
On bias accusations, see R.4.13n.
A case management justice has just as much power as a justice doing other things, and can
decide discrete issues or hear summary judgment applications, except that the justice cannot
conduct a trial without consent. The form or title of a paper commencing or requesting the hearing
is not determinative. And of course the pleadings and the law and Rules must be followed.¹
A case management judge can make an interim order even shortly before trial, to guard
the interests of a trial.²
The courts resist fragmentation of proceedings and piecemeal appellate
review, and case management directions can usually be appealed at the end of the trial if they
had effect through to that state. The essence of case management is to provide coherence,
predictability, and stability.³
The case management judge knows and can record what happened in front of him or her,
and can later analyze whether the judge’s directions have been properly carried out, so if a formal
order is not promptly prepared by counsel, the judge may take care of the record.⁴
Case management judges can vary their previous procedural orders, schedules, and litigation
plans, as circumstances change, e. g. by continuing refusal to obey court orders.⁵
There is no rule
that once a case management judge directs that a particular procedure be followed, he or she
cannot vary that procedure, adopt a different procedure, vary a litigation plan, or alter time limits
and other procedural directions.⁶
Rule 1.5(1) and (4) deal with procedural irregularities and imply
that variations from a litigation plan or litigation protocol can be cured. The key issue is whether
there is any prejudice arising from the irregularity or non-compliance.⁷
Permission was given to appeal a refusal by a case management judge to adjourn the trial,
shortly after the party seeking adjournment said that he was taken by surprise by an expert
valuation report just served by the other side.⁸

2020 amendments to Rule 1.4 lets a case management judge or case conference judge order
a private alternate dispute resolution. Here a private mediation of ﬁnancial issues was ordered.¹
After the court directed that a family chambers special date be booked and used, and the
parties do book it, that leaves no license to expand or ignore the issues which the court directed.
Parties cannot prioritize their own (respective) litigation strategies, by raising additional issues,
nor try to avoid issues which the court had directed. That is doubly so if the parties agreed that the
matter was urgent. Parties should either ﬁle the application which they were directed to ﬁle, or tell
the court at once if they wish to abandon those issues. So the sole issue was what was directed.²
A case management judge gets deference on appeal.³
Especially when he or she must
balance many competing factors.⁴
Deviations from the litigation plans or protocols adopted by
the case management judge will not be interfered with on appeal unless they reﬂect an error in
principle, are clearly unreasonable, or result in manifest unfairness to one of the parties.⁵
In order
to establish a reviewable error in varying a litigation plan or litigation protocol, the appellant would
need to demonstrate prejudice not compensable in costs or for which he or she cannot seek a
suitable remedy.⁶
Fair notice and a chance for each party, and a sufﬁcient record, are needed to make a
substantive decision.⁷
The appellant claimed that he did not know that any substantive topics would be decided
during the upcoming case management hearing now under appeal; but that was incorrect, as one
of the matters for that session was an outstanding formal application by the other parties, and that
topic was mentioned during oral argument.⁸

R.4.14(3) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
Even in case management, a judge should not decide an issue without looking at an afﬁdavit
touching on the question ﬁled by a party, even one ﬁled just before the hearing.¹
A case management Judge can vary a procedure or plan which he or she has ordered,
including time limits, and any judge can waive irregularities.²
Ordinarily it takes evidence to have an earlier order changed. A case management judge who
had ordered expert examination of a computer seized under an Anton Piller order could rely on
the expert’s report, even if the expert did not look for certain types of problem: he was not ordered
to look for them, and no one had suggested or asked to have them researched.³
Though evidence
normally must be sworn, where a case management judge has run a piece of litigation for some
years and there is a counsel for the child who reports what communications he or she had with
one of the parties, it may be proper for the case management judge to make a procedural order
based on that.⁴
A case management judge’s decision that a ﬁnal settlement was reached, can be
based on his having watched most of the negotiations, and it attracts appellate deference.⁵
The
case management judge can ask for, get, and rely on, letters from counsel or their submissions,
about the progress of the suit, what is outstanding and what is still needed, especially when an
afﬁdavit of records lists much of this, all without requiring a (new) afﬁdavit about this. And that
judge can look at court records and previous orders, in this suit.⁶
A case management Judge’s letter “escorting” a formal order which the Judge signed said
that a new application would be needed if a new pleading was still desired, and an earlier order
had required leave for any more pleadings. The letter changed nothing and was not a new ruling.⁷
Also on case management judges, see the Weatherford case.⁸
Both sides later asked the case management judge to conduct an early judicial dispute
resolution, and did. It did not settle the suit, and then one party complained that the judge could
no longer do case management, because he was a former judicial dispute resolution judge. The
complaint was rejected because of the knowledge, requests and consents.⁹
A pre-trial conference necessary to get a trial date can lead to a fee under Schedule C item
9, trial readiness.¹⁰
A case management judge can rule on whether an expert report is admissible at an upcoming
trial, but should do so cautiously, and not where the trial judge would be in a substantially better
position to rule.¹¹

See Kyle v. Kyle.¹
Sometimes “case management” is a term used loosely, but under the Rules, it is a formal
written application under R.4.12 to the Chief Justice to designate a judge to take the whole action
under case management. If there was no such application or order here, R.4.15 restricting who
can hear applications, or who can be trial judge, has no application (unless and until there is such
an application order).²

DIVISION 3
DISPUTE RESOLUTION BY AGREEMENT
Subdivision 1
Dispute Resolution Processes
Dispute Resolution Processes
4.16(1) The responsibility of the parties to manage their dispute includes good
faith participation in one or more of the following dispute resolution processes
with respect to all or any part of the action:
(a) a dispute resolution process in the private or government sectors
involving an impartial third person;
(b) a Court annexed dispute resolution process;
(c) a judicial dispute resolution process described in rules 4.17 to 4.21
[Judicial Dispute Resolution];
(d) any program or process designated by the Court for the purpose of
this rule.
(2) On application, the Court may waive the responsibility of the parties under
this rule, but only if
(a) before the action started the parties engaged in a dispute resolution
process and the parties and the Court believe that a further dispute
resolution process would not be beneﬁcial,

Footnote
1 R.4.10n.
2 Dow Chem. Can. v. NOVA Chem. Corp. (#_) 2024 ABKB 98, JCC 0601 07921 (Feb 21) (¶’s 17-25).

4-18
R.4.16(3) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
(b) the nature of the claim is not one, in all the circumstances, that will
or is likely to result in an agreement between the parties,
(c) there is a compelling reason why a dispute resolution process
should not be attempted by the parties,
(d) the Court is satisﬁed that engaging in a dispute resolution process
would be futile, or
(e) the claim is of such a nature that a decision by the Court is necessary
or desirable.
(3) The parties must attend the hearing of an application under subrule (2) unless
the Court otherwise orders.
(4) A case management judge or a case conference judge may, on application
or on the Court’s own motion, by order direct that the parties participate in a
dispute resolution process.
(5) In determining whether an order under subrule (4) should be made, the
case management judge or case conference judge may consider all relevant
circumstances, including
(a) the issues in the litigation,
(b) the nature of the cause of action and the relief claimed,
(c) the identity, relationship and means of the parties,
(d) whether the action has proceeded to a stage at which alternative
dispute resolution is likely to be successful, including whether
record production and questioning are sufficiently advanced to
support the dispute resolution process, and
(e) whether any of the factors in subrule (2) justify delaying, modifying
or dispensing with the need to participate in a dispute resolution
process.
(6) As part of an order made under subrule (4), the case management judge or
case conference judge may give directions respecting any aspect of the dispute
resolution process, including
(a) the identity of a neutral third party to be involved in the process,
(b) where an equal sharing of the expenses is not appropriate, directions
apportioning the responsibility of each party for the expenses of the
neutral third party and other disbursements relating to the process,
(c) the time, location, structure or conduct of the process, and
(d) the consequences of the failure of any party to comply with any
directions, or to pay its share of the expenses.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;36/20]
(Subrules 4-6 were effective Mar 27, 2020. )
Effective March 17, 2020, amendments inserted new subrules (4) - (6) into R.4.16, letting a
case management judge or case conference judge order a private alternate dispute resolution. So

4-19
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.16(6)
a private mediation of ﬁnancial issues was ordered. If the parties could not agree on a mediator,
the judge would pick one. It could be electronic so it could proceed during the Covid epidemic.¹
A Court of King's Bench notice to the profession of September 4, 2020 is about mandatory
alternative dispute resolution. It is found in the “News and Announcements” of the Court of King's
Bench website.
Neither the Arbitration Act nor the Rules of Court let the court impose on a party ﬁnal binding
arbitration if that party does not consent. The Rules are all about the parties’ engagement of the
court. Rule 4.16 speaks about a private sector impartial third person, but the Rules do not speak
about arbitration, which is not “in or by a court process”. What a judge can direct is non-binding
dispute resolution. Arbitration is binding. Nor does the Divorce Act say the court can impose
arbitration on a non-consenting party or parties.²
For some practical guidance on when to mediate, how to mediate, and how you and your client
should behave during mediation, see Côté, Systematic Advocacy, Chap.11 (Juriliber 2017).
The 2010 Rules make dispute resolution mandatory, and the court initially refused to dispense
with it.³
Suspension of enforcement of mandatory Judicial Dispute Resolution was lifted as part
of a one year pilot project September 1, 2019 and then the pilot project was “extended” and
mandatory Judicial Dispute Resolution is still required. But in practice, Judicial Dispute Resolutions
for matters other than family matters are rarely accessed. The booking procedure states that civil
matters (other than family) must be ready for trial and attach a ﬁled or unﬁled Form 37 executed
by all parties.
Parties cannot get R.4.16 waived just for the asking, nor by consent. A judicial dispute
resolution might at least clarify the issues, and give the parties the beneﬁt of an independent
opinion about the merits.⁴
The court stressed the foundational Rule and the fact that the 1968
Rules had no such provision, and quoted criteria from Ontario for requiring or waiving judicial
dispute resolution. Strength of the plaintiff’s claim is not enough. Semble futility is not enough to
waive? Nor is intransigence of one party. Requiring a judicial dispute resolution will often produce
an unexpected settlement. The aim is not just large movement by both sides. Sometimes it is to
persuade one side that it will likely lose a trial.⁵
The need for dispute resolution process before trial
was not waived in a domestic case.⁶
But then a shortage of judges and overwork forced the Court of King's Bench to stop enforcing
Rr.8.4(3) (a) and 8.5(1) (a).⁷
There is empirical evidence which shows that compulsory mediation can be effective and even
settle a suit, despite lack of consent by one or both parties.⁸

4-20
R.4.16(6) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
Refusal to participate in dispute resolution is contrary to the foundational Rules and the modern
culture shift, so here costs were therefore increased to a lump sum about 80% of full indemnity.¹
Limitation periods apply to arbitration proceedings, and an interpretation that would have
made them meaningless was rejected. That means that if within the limitation period a statement
of claim is issued but nothing is done to start arbitration, the claim is dead.²
Judicial Dispute Resolution is designed to help the parties resolve part or all of a claim by
agreement. Here at the outset the parties agreed that if they did not reach agreement to resolve
the matter, the judge would make a determination ﬁnal and binding on all parties. If a judge at
such a Judicial Dispute Resolution issues a ﬁnal order after the process, is it a consent order if at
the time of creation it was not clear that all parties expressly consented to each speciﬁc element
in that order? The actual words of the applicable Rules matter, and words there should not lightly
be assumed to be surplusage with no effect. Nor is a Judicial Dispute Resolution arbitration.³
The Alberta Court of Justice has no inherent power; only superior courts do. The Alberta Court
of Justice gets power only from statutes, properly interpreted. The Rules of Court do not give
inherent jurisdiction to any court, given s.96 of the 1867 Constitution. The statutory power of the
Alberta Court of Justice to order mediation requires ﬁling of a dispute note. And mediation is not
a mere procedure or practice in which the Rules could ﬁll a gap. A private mediation is something
entirely different.⁴
There is a discussion of binding JDRs, which are created by an agreement between the
parties, not imposed by the court or the Rules of Court or the law (though the judge does act in
a judicial capacity). One reason they have been created is that some litigants will not invest the
time and money to prepare for a JDR if it may produce no ﬁnal resolution.⁵
Often the agreement
simpliﬁes later enforcement of the decision by giving the judge power to sign a consent judgment
to the same effect as the award.⁶
An appeal from a binding JDR is almost impossible, practically speaking, because leave to
appeal is needed, there was no sworn evidence, no transcripts are available, and exhibits are not
formally entered and marked. Fact ﬁndings or other reasons may well not be set out, and what
would be a reviewable error by the judge is hard to know or prove.⁷
That the agreement for the
binding JDR says no appeal is possible is irrelevant, as appeal rights come from statute and a
contract cannot remove them.⁸
Permission to appeal would be hard to get as well, especially as
rules of evidence or procedure do not apply, subject to some need for basic fairness.⁹
When a judge has (by agreement) heard and decided a binding JDR, the juustice may become
functus eventually, but he or she can clear up ambiguities in the award, and probably cannot
become functus before an entered ﬁnal judgment.¹⁰

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.17
Information Note
Note that under rule 8.4(3) [Trial date: scheduled by court clerk], the court clerk cannot
schedule a trial date unless satisfactory evidence is produced that the parties have
participated in a dispute resolution process or the Court, by order, waives this requirement
under rule 4.16(2). If the Court sets a trial date under rule 8.5 [Trial date: scheduled by the
Court] the Court may, if the conditions of rule 4.16(2) are met, give a waiver of that rule at
that time.

Related Provisions
4.2 (what the responsibility to manage litigation includes)

Subdivision 2
Judicial Dispute Resolution
Purpose of Judicial Dispute Resolution
4.17 The purpose of this Subdivision is to provide a party-initiated framework for
a judge to actively facilitate a process in which the parties resolve all or part of
a claim by agreement.

Related Provisions
10.31(2) (c) (no costs for ADR).

This phrase is often abbreviated as “J. D. R. ” There are textbooks on this subject.¹
The “News and Announcements” part of the Court of King's Bench website has a prominent
link entitled “Judicial Dispute Resolution”. Clicking on that yields a list of past notices to the
profession, each of which can be downloaded. They are arranged in date order, from April, 2020.
Most are directly about judicial dispute resolution, and a few others may be of some use to parties
or Counsel working in that area.
Civil judicial dispute resolution is difﬁcult to obtain, so R.4.16 effectively mandates private
mediation at the expense of the parties.
For a family law suit, a judge appointed a “case management counsel” to meet with parties
and carry between them any proposal, and ordered the parties to meet with that counsel. One
party made unfounded accusations of serious misconduct against that counsel, for which the
judge severely criticized that party and imposed a penalty payable to the Clerk of the Court.²

4.18(1) An arrangement for a judicial dispute resolution process may be made
only with the agreement of the participating parties and, before engaging in a
judicial dispute resolution process, and subject to the directions of the presiding

judge, the participating parties must agree to the extent possible on at least the
following:
(a) that every party necessary to participate in the process has agreed

(b) rules to be followed in the process, including rules respecting
(i) the nature of the process,

(2) The parties who agree on the proposed judicial dispute resolution
process are entitled to participate in the process.
(3) The parties to a proposed judicial dispute resolution process may request
that a judge named by the parties participate in the process.
Information Note
The parties to a JDR process cannot, of course, bind a judge to participate in the process.
The parties should find out in advance whether their proposed process gives a judge any
difficulty. If the judge is not willing to participate in the process agreed on by the parties, the
parties are free to seek the assistance of another judge.
If the parties agree and a judge is willing, the judge may assist the parties in working out a
JDR process.

judge can act); 14.60 (dispute resolution on appeal).
The Court of King’s Bench has been issuing public notices about booking judicial dispute
resolution sessions under the “News & Announcements” part of the Court of King’s Bench website.
One published December 14, 2022 was for the January-June 2023 term and presumably others
were to be issued for later terms. This one lists judges, available dates, and names priorities for
which cases will be selected.

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.19
In a binding Judicial Dispute Resolution the one judge acted both as an arbitrator and as a
judge, but that does not give any reasonable apprehension of bias, and R.4.21 is not on that
point. And the party impliedly waived any concern about that by this initial agreement. A judge is
presumed to act judicially and to disabuse her mind of any matters learned which the law says to
ignore. Independent legal advice before settling may be wise, but is not legally required.¹
Documents Resulting From Judicial Dispute Resolution
4.19 The only documents, if any, that may result from a judicial dispute resolution
process are
(a) an agreement prepared by the parties, and any other document
necessary to implement the agreement,
(b) a consent order or consent judgment resulting from the process,
and
(c) a transcript of proceedings made in open court at the time of the
judicial dispute resolution process which records the outcome of
the judicial dispute resolution process.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;128/15]

Related Provisions
5.8n (privilege) ; 5.25 (scope of questioning).

After a judicial dispute resolution conference, one party contended that the result was binding.
The parties reached no agreement, nor was there a consent order or consent judgment. Only
an agreement prepared by the parties, or a consent order or consent judgment resulting, is a
document that may result from a judicial dispute resolution.²
Conﬁdentiality and Use of Information
4.20(1) A judicial dispute resolution process is a conﬁdential process intended
to facilitate the resolution of a dispute.
(2) Unless the parties otherwise agree in writing, statements made or documents
generated for or in the judicial dispute resolution process with a view to resolving
the dispute
(a) are privileged and are made or generated without prejudice,
(b) must be treated by the parties and participants in the process as
conﬁdential and may only be used for the purpose of that dispute
resolution process, and
(c) may not be referred to, presented as evidence or relied on, and are
not admissible in a subsequent application or proceeding in the
same action or in any other action, or in proceedings of a judicial or
quasi-judicial nature.

1 Shannon v. Shannon 2023 ABCA 79, Calg 2101 0069 AC (Mar 6) (¶’s 49-59).

(4) Subrule (2) does not prevent the use of statements made or documents
generated for or in the judicial dispute resolution process to prove the fact that
a settlement was reached or the terms of a settlement.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;128/15]

Admitting a draft bill of costs at a judicial dispute resolution session is without prejudice and

The conﬁdentiality of a judicial dispute resolution process or an “E. I. C. C. ” in Family Docket
Court, can be waived by both parties’ giving evidence as to the agreement made.²
Involvement of Judge After Process Concludes
4.21(1) The judge facilitating a judicial dispute resolution process in an action
must not hear or decide any subsequent application, proceeding or trial in the
action without the written agreement of every party and the agreement of the
judge.
(2) The judge facilitating a judicial dispute resolution process must treat the
judicial dispute resolution process as conﬁdential, and all the records relating
to the process in the possession of the judge or in the possession of the court
clerk must be returned to the parties or destroyed except
(a) the agreement of the parties and any document necessary to
implement the agreement, and
(b) a consent order or consent judgment resulting from the process.
(3) The judge facilitating a judicial dispute resolution process is not competent
to give evidence nor compellable to give evidence in any application or
proceeding relating to the process in the same action, in any other action, or in
any proceeding of a judicial or quasi–judicial nature.
See Kyle v. Kyle.³
Any JDR, binding or not, is conﬁdential and without prejudice, and R.4.19 limits the record
available.⁴

4-25
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.22
DIVISION 4
SECURITY FOR PAYMENT OF COSTS AWARD
Considerations for Security for Costs Order
4.22 The Court may order a party to provide security for payment of a costs
award if the Court considers it just and reasonable to do so, taking into account
all of the following:
(a) whether it is likely the applicant for the order will be able to enforce
an order or judgment against assets in Alberta;
(b) the ability of the respondent to the application to pay the costs
award;
(c) the merits of the action in which the application is ﬁled;
(d) whether an order to give security for payment of a costs award would
unduly prejudice the respondent’s ability to continue the action;
(e) any other matter the Court considers appropriate.¹

Related Provisions
Business Corporations Act, RSA 2000, c. B.9 s.254; 5.3(2) (a) (security for costs for

discovery); 10.30 (when a costs award may be made) ; 12.36 (security for costs in family law) ;
14.67 (security for costs on appeal).
A. Introduction
1. References
There are relevant textbooks.²
2. General Approach
This topic is important, because costs are the important control measure on quantity and
intensity of litigation, so someone who cannot or will not pay costs is free from such controls.³
Access to justice is not the same as access without fear of costs consequences.⁴
Freedom from
any real danger of paying costs means no incentive to end the suit, no matter how uneconomical
or weak it may be, and an impecunious company is a one-way value for costs.⁵

Footnote
1 Quite similar to previous 1968 R.593. It came from 1914 R.722, and 1944 C. R.720. Cf. Judic. Ord. R.520
and English O.65 r.6A.
2 Generally, see the C. P. E. , Chapter 54; Hals. Laws of Canada, Civil Procedure, 907 ff. (2d reissue 2012) ;
Abrams and McGuinness, Canadian Civil Procedure Law, 997 ff. (2d ed.2010).
3 Stoney v. T’ee. of the 1985 Sawridge Tr.2017 ABCA 437, Edm 1703 0195 AC (Dec 19). Such as a company
with no assets: Owners Condo. Plan 982 6403 v. CPI Crown Prop. Int. Corp.2018 ABCA 232, 76 Alta
LR(6th) 318 (one JA) ((¶’s 22-32).
4 MUA v. Four Points Sheraton Edm. South (M) 2017 ABQB 804, JCE 1703 01982 (Dec 21) (¶’s 26 ff. ).
5 Blazer Mechan. Plbg. &c. v. Delnor Constr.2024 ABKB 183, JCC 1801 14036 (Apr 2) (¶’s 47-51).

4-26
R.4.22 PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
The present tests for security for costs are much laxer and more ﬂexible than they were a few
years ago.¹
The 2010 Rule drops the categories of facts found in all the earlier Rules, and focusses more
on whether the order for security is just and reasonable, if it is R.4.22 which governs.²
Indeed even
the factors mentioned in the Rules are not exhaustive.³
There is a review of the factors to be used.⁴
The conﬂicting factors for and against security for costs must be balanced and viewed objectively,
considering the interests of all parties.⁵
That is important, as some chambers decisions seem to
look wrongly only at possible inconvenience or danger to the plaintiff.⁶
The 2010 Rules let the court look at the merits of both sides’ cases,⁷
but no careful or deep
weighing is called for,⁸
only very clear cases of weakness or great strength?⁹
And probably not
even that; the court should not try to decide which party’s case is stronger.¹⁰
Indeed the court really
cannot do that at this early stage in the lawsuit.¹¹
3. Mistaken Hostility to Security
Some recent decisions, especially in other provinces, have taken a hostile view of security for
costs, hinted that it should be rare, and raised picky excuses not to order it. That is not the law
of Alberta.¹²
It is highly doubtful that the basic principles have changed. Defendants should be
protected from plaintiffs with nothing to lose, or who are immune from execution.¹³
Or from a party
who wants the remedies and protection of Alberta courts, yet disavows in advance any possible
collection from him.¹⁴

4-27
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.22
4. Security from a Defendant
The Alberta Rule is not limited to ordering a plaintiff to give security. But it takes exceptional
circumstances for the court to make a defendant give security for costs to the plaintiff.¹
Mortgages
given in the ordinary course of business, and suing the plaintiff’s lawyer, do not sufﬁce, even if the
defendant has no exigible assets here.²
Lack of assets here is more pardonable if the defendant
does not live here; he or she did not choose this forum; the plaintiff did.³
5. Security as a Condition of Other Relief
Security for costs is also commonly given as a condition of some other relief. If so, that is done
under R.1.42(e), and the tests or preconditions in R.4.22 need not be met.⁴
Rule 1.42(e) is a
general Rule, and explains why other more speciﬁc Rules, such as R.7.3, no longer expressly
provide for conditions.⁵
For instance, security from a party which has missed deadlines getting time extended,⁶
or
a party seeking special relief (such as an interlocutory injunction), or a defendant who escapes
pre-trial attachment,⁷
or someone who barely escapes summary judgment (or dismissal), or just
barely gets his or her default opened up,⁸
or a plaintiff who just escapes a vexatious litigant order
barring further suits.⁹
See also Rr.14.67 (appeals), and 12.36 (divorces). If grounds for an Anton
Piller order are shaky, and the plaintiffs may be judgment-proof here, they may be required to post
heavy security.¹⁰
In a family matter where one party suddenly says that any trial will last much longer and cost
more than earlier agreed or forecast because it will involve many more witnesses, and that most
or all of the new witnesses must testify live and cannot put in their evidence by afﬁdavits, the court
may give him the option of either trimming the trial down to a reasonable size, or putting up some
security for costs to reﬂect the extra expense of the additional length.¹¹
Giving relief or letting the plaintiff do something conditional on paying costs ordered up to
this point, is possible and analogous to security for costs, but it requires balancing the competing
factors, and tailoring the amount to be paid now to the parties’ means.¹²
6. Corporations
An impecunious company is a one-way valve for costs, and so makes security important.¹³

R.4.22 PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
The Business Corporations Act gives a freestanding right to security which may be used in
those situations.¹
Security for costs can be granted under either R.4.22 or under the Alberta
Business Corporations Act. It is not necessary to satisfy the tests under both. Rule 4.22 is
addressed to all litigants.²
This section in the Act is discretionary, but it does require evidence that
the company will be unable to pay the costs.³
Section 254 of the Business Corporations Act applies only to suits by an incorporated body,
not to an individual’s claim or giving security.⁴
It should be used, not this Rule, if the plaintiff is a
company?⁵
The relation between that Act and this Rule is unclear at the moment. Do they use different
tests,⁶
or are the two pieces of legislation to be read together harmoniously?⁷
A company can be
ordered to give security by satisfying either s.254 of the Act, or R.4.22.⁸
But of course the statute
cannot be used to make a defendant give security to the plaintiff.⁹
It is easier for a defendant sued by a company to get an order for security for costs if the
defendant has requested from the company some easier type of security, e. g. a personal promise
or guarantee from an individual.¹⁰
B. Relevant Factors
1. Discretion
Security is discretionary.¹¹
The Court can look at all the circumstances,¹²
and refuse to order
security if it would be unjust.¹³
For example, the plaintiff may have a strong case, have no way of

Irreparable harm to the respondent seeking security need not be shown, and is not a relevant

2. Plaintiff’s Lack of Funds
The plaintiff’s lack of money is very often not itself enough reason to deny security, and indeed
can be among the reasons to give it,³
particularly if the plaintiff has other assets,⁴
or has any way
of raising funds.⁵
And doubly so if it is a society or company.⁶
The court should note whether a company professing poverty in order to resist giving security,
has been continuing to litigate for some time, including a number of interlocutory motions.⁷

R.4.22 PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
The standard of proof of inability to pay costs is probability only.¹
The issue is exigible assets
in Alberta, and failure to pay previous costs is some evidence of that.²
A closely-held company can at least provide personal guarantees from its shareholders.³
Lack
of cash on hand or income is not a sufﬁcient bar to a security order; the person alleging poverty
must show that he or she or it cannot raise security, e. g. by a loan or gift from someone else.⁴
An allegation that the plaintiff cannot raise money or security must be clearly proved.⁵
Undue
prejudice to the plaintiff from ordering security cannot be found if the plaintiff gives no evidence
of inability to put up security.⁶
A brief conclusory statement in the plaintiff’s afﬁdavit is not enough;
details are necessary.⁷
Indeed then the plaintiff must provide a fair degree of ﬁnancial disclosure,
including income, assets, liabilities, and ability to borrow.⁸
Of course the defendant must show an arguable defence: see Part C.3 below.
3. Plaintiff’s Misconduct
That the plaintiff has not paid costs formerly awarded against him or her is also relevant.⁹
Indeed it can be almost compelling.¹⁰
If the plaintiff makes grave allegations against the defendants, then the costs could be large if
the allegations are not proved; that is also relevant.¹¹
A litigation terrorist who always refused to pay costs assessed against him, and who had
been barred by the Court of Appeal until he paid all outstanding costs awards, received further
sanctions in the Court of King’s Bench.¹²
They were $10 thousand security for costs for future
matters, a penalty of $2500 for abusive relitigation during the leave process, and proof of payment
of all outstanding penalties in King’s Bench under R.10.49(1) (including one above).

4-31
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.22
4. Merits of the Suit
The court can look at the merits of the lawsuit,¹
in order to exercise its discretion.²
Merits are
not made more relevant by calling them “public policy”.³
But the perceived strength or weakness
of the plaintiff’s case is not enough alone.⁴
If the plaintiff’s case is novel and would face an uphill
battle, that is relevant.⁵
But the court does not try to decide which side’s case is stronger.⁶
No in-depth analysis of the
merits of the claim or the defence is necessary,⁷
indeed it is undesirable; who has the stronger
case is not the question.⁸
If the plaintiff has a habit of reckless litigation with poor results, that reinforces the case for
security.⁹
Sometimes the claim seems to have merit and the plaintiff may not have funds to put up
security. The latter is sometimes said to be something to consider,¹⁰
but much less so when the
plaintiff is a company; then the question is whether those controlling it could help it come up with
some kind of security.¹¹
C. Procedure
1. Timing
It is doubtful that moving too early or too late is an absolute bar to seeking security for costs.¹²

But since security need not be ordered, the court can take into account all the circumstances,
including timing.¹
If the defendant lets the plaintiff run up a lot of disbursements without any
demand for security, that may be a ground to conﬁne security to future costs, not past ones.²
If
the plaintiff knew all along that security would be sought, a later order need not be conﬁned to
future steps’ costs.³
And if the party opposing more security had asked the court to order more
interlocutory steps, security for costs of past steps is proper.⁴
But delay in moving may be excused if the need for security was not apparent earlier,⁵
and
there is no prejudice from the delay.⁶
And delay by the plaintiff in the suit may be a reason to
excuse the defendant’s delay in seeking security.⁷
Fourteen years after the court denied security,
the suit had still not got to trial; the plaintiff's delay and paying other creditors and changes in the
Rules of Court were new circumstances, and security was ordered.⁸
2. Demand for Security
Generally, a prior demand for security is customary and wise.⁹
Notice of some sort is necessary
before an order for security.¹⁰
3. Arguable Defence
Security from the plaintiff usually would be unjust if there were no arguable defence to the
action; so in all cases the defendant seeking security should swear an afﬁdavit to show that
the defendant has an arguable defence.¹¹
Non-afﬁdavit evidence may not sufﬁce alone.¹²
It is not

enough just to swear to that conclusion of an arguable defence; the defendant should swear to
facts which disclose such a defence.¹
But the defence need only be arguable, not ironclad,²
or even weighty.³
Oddly, the 1968 Rule
requiring an afﬁdavit disclosing a defence and requiring the facts creating the defence to be sworn
to ﬁrsthand, not by hearsay, is now gone. Rule 4.22(c) speaking of “the merits” cannot literally be
complied with at an early stage, and it requires no more than 1968 R.594 did.⁴
If the afﬁdavit lacks a simple fact which is likely true, the defendant may get an adjournment to
supply a supplementary afﬁdavit.⁵
4. Miscellaneous
Whether to give security for costs involves a two-step process.⁶
There is urgency in an appeal from an order for security for costs.⁷
Partly for that reason,
permission to appeal such an order is required, but hard to get.⁸
An unanticipated motion for security does not undo the moving party’s duty to answer
examination for discovery questions or to obey undertakings given on examination for discovery
(questioning).⁹
Where there are cross-applications, often it is best to hear the application for
security for costs ﬁrst.¹⁰
D. Exigible Assets
If the defendant makes out a good case on its face for security for costs, indeed merely
shows that the plaintiff company will be unable to pay costs, then the onus shifts to the plaintiff
to show that security is unnecessary.¹¹

A vague assertion of assets does not sufﬁce.²

enough, in a big lawsuit.⁸

In looking at the basket clause of factors relevant to security, a matter to weigh is that the
plaintiff led no evidence.¹
The onus is the same whether the plaintiff is an individual or a company.²
Even if neither side gives any real evidence of the plaintiff’s ﬁnancial state or assets, under s.

Footnote
254 of the Business Corporations Act, often the onus of showing such evidence is on the party
seeking security for costs. (It could be different if the suit is huge and complex and the plaintiff is
a recently-created non-resident corporation). Sec.254 forces the defendant moving to give some
evidence that the plaintiff corporation will be unable to pay costs.³
Maybe the onus is different
under R.4.22, but that Rule was not relied on in the Polypropylene case.⁴
Whether the plaintiff can be cross-examined on his afﬁdavit resisting security and on what
issues and to what depth, are discussed at length in the Bechir case.⁵
E. Counterclaims etc.
A plaintiff by counterclaim might have to give security, especially if the counterclaim in
substance was more than just a defence to the main claim.⁶
Still more if the plaintiff does not
propose to proceed with the main claim.⁷
A counterclaim or cross claim by the defendant is not
enough to bar security for costs.⁸
So a defence of set-off pleaded by counterclaim would not attract
security for costs against the plaintiff by counterclaim.⁹
Mere overlap between the statement of
defence and the counterclaim is not a bar to security.¹⁰
Mere existence of a counterclaim does
not bar security for costs; the issue is how interwoven the main claim and counterclaim are,¹¹
and
whether the counterclaim adds much to length or complexity.¹²
A counterclaim was still to be tried, though the main claim was decided. The plaintiff by
counterclaim (defendant on main claim) had to post security not only for costs, but for the unpaid
judgment on the main claim.¹³
The cases on security in third party proceedings are split.¹⁴

R.4.22 PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
F. Amount of Security
1. Basic Approach
Costs awards are often inadequate, and the amount of security for costs is often even more
inadequate. Party-party costs were sometimes a ﬁfth or a tenth of actual solicitor-client costs,
especially for pre-trial steps, and in the past, security for costs was often only a fraction of a low
estimate of party-party costs. That was the worst kind of tokenism. Then ﬁnally a long-standing
amendment to the Rules increased Schedule C somewhat, but inﬂation since it was drafted has
again eroded Schedule C signiﬁcantly.
Older cases using fractions of Schedule C should not be followed, and security should be at,
or close to, an estimate of likely costs should the suit fail.¹
The justice or Applications Judge should
calculate usual or likely party-party costs for all steps in the suit. , e. g. on the appropriate column
of Schedule C, and award that.²
If solicitor-client costs are quite possible, the security should be
so calculated.³
Each of two separate defendants got security, and it was based on triple column 5 of Schedule
C, as the plaintiff claimed far above $1.5 million.⁴
If the plaintiff claims large amounts, it is reasonable to expect the defendant to take thorough
steps in its defence.⁵
If ultimate solicitor-client costs are possible, but might not be awarded, the security may be
calculated on an enhanced party-party basis.⁶
If legislation bars security in oppression suits, in a suit partly for oppression, what would be a
proper amount can then be reduced.⁷

Footnote

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.22
2. Past Steps
Security for costs is not limited to costs for future steps only,¹
especially when a company
which seemed solvent has since sold its assets and gone out of business and there was no delay
in applying once the need for security became evident.²
Previous unpaid costs were added to half
an estimate of future costs (multiplying Schedule C) to calculate security.³
3. Evidence
Where the party-party costs alleged by the defendant seeking security will be largely based
upon Schedule C, then no evidence of them is needed, and a pro forma bill of costs will sufﬁce.⁴
However, if special circumstances or heavy disbursements are suggested (e. g. for experts),
then there should be afﬁdavit evidence of the likely amounts.⁵
An offer of settlement cannot be
used for that purpose.⁶
A receiver sought security calculated at $65, 993, assuming reasonable
and efﬁcient proceedings. The judge was used to that receivership and the nature and scope of
previous applications by the self-represented opposing party, and concluded that $100 thousand
would be a more reasonable estimate.⁷
4. Time and Form of Security
See also R.4.23n.
Security for costs is presumed all to be posted now, absent evidence that that is unnecessary
or too onerous.⁸
But ordering payment in instalments, stage by stage in the lawsuit, is often fair to
both sides, especially if the plaintiff is short of funds at present.⁹
An undertaking by the owners of a small company to be personally liable for costs, may be
ordered as security for costs.¹⁰
An appeal court should not ﬁne tune the amount of security ordered
in King's Bench.¹¹
That the plaintiff’s case seems strong is an illogical reason to reduce the amount
of the security.¹²
A nominal amount of security was ordered where there was a dispute over whether the
plaintiff’s impecuniosity was caused by the defendant’s actions, and the Chambers Justice could
not get to the bottom of that.¹³
Where the corporate plaintiff sues many people and has no viable business, and mobile
assets, and some claims are dubious, the fact that it cannot put up an adequate total pool of

security, still less pay the potential costs of all its litigation, means that each defendant is entitled
to some security (more for suits further advanced), to produce a big total amount of security.¹
G. Speciﬁc Grounds for Security
1. Different Types of Plaintiff
If there is more than one plaintiff, and one plaintiff is within the class of parties who by statute
may be ordered to give security, can that be ordered if the other plaintiff is not within that class?
The decided cases were split, but the better answer seems to be yes.²
In a given case, maybe
no glib answer sufﬁces; the court must carefully analyze the true risk of nonpayment of any costs
ordered, and even see if the solvent plaintiff will agree to be jointly and severally liable for any
costs.³
If there are related suits, partly parallel and partly cross, security may be ordered in the
second suit if it will require much work not needed in the ﬁrst suit.⁴
It must be recalled that under
the current Rules, security no longer has any rigid preconditions, only some relevant things to
weigh.
Does the fact that there are a great many plaintiffs militate against security being ordered?⁵
2. Non-Residents
A very common ground for seeking security used to be the fact that the plaintiff resided outside
Alberta.⁶
Alberta case law makes irrelevant the fact that the plaintiff resides in a reciprocating
jurisdiction for enforcement of “foreign” judgments.⁷
Three non-resident plaintiffs may not have to
give security for costs, if one plaintiff lives in Alberta.⁸
The main question was where the plaintiff resided, which implies some small degree of habit.
It is not the same as domicile, nor temporary presence, nor carrying on business, nor place
of incorporation. But those things could be evidence, or relevant to presence of assets.⁹
For a
company, the question is location of its central direction and control.¹⁰
An insolvent estate of a non-
resident defendant may have to give security.¹¹
That the plaintiff carries on no business here and
does not allege that its assets are here, puts the onus of proof onto the plaintiff resisting security.¹²

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.22
3. Unpaid Costs
At one time, unpaid costs were not themselves a ground for security unless they were in the
same cause as the present one, and the defendant moving for security bore the onus of proving
their identity.¹
But now that there are no ﬁxed fact categories, the proposition in the previous
sentence may no longer be law.²
But a number of unpaid costs orders in the same lawsuit are grounds for security.³
4. Nominal Plaintiff
A nominal plaintiff may have to put up security. That applies to one who has assigned away all
the future fruits of the suit.⁴
5. Frivolous Suit
The 1968 Rule speciﬁcally listed a frivolous and vexatious suit as a basis for a security for
costs order, but the evidence of that had to come from examination of the plaintiff, which narrowed
it a good deal.⁵
There are no such restrictions now. Transcripts showing that the plaintiff’s case
seems weak, and confusion, may well sufﬁce, especially if the plaintiff is tying up the defendant’s
property.⁶
6. Corporate Plaintiff
See also Parts A.6, B.2, and D above.
The court should consider relevant statutes.⁷
The one most commonly relied on is the Alberta
Business Corporations Act, s.245. That applies to any body corporate suing which will be unable
to pay the costs of a successful defendant.
At one time, the defendant had to do more than ﬁt the plaintiff within that provision; the
defendant also had to satisfy the Rules.⁸
Probably that is no longer true: if one ﬁts within the
Business Corporations Act, that is enough to get security.⁹
The defendant need not prove conclusively that the body corporate could not pay costs; all
that the court needs is good reason to think it could not.¹⁰
That the plaintiff has no assets here

and is not carrying on business is enough.¹
So is failure to pay judgments, or costs previously
ordered?²
So is receivership or bankruptcy or winding up.³
Bankruptcy with few assets and a
trustee in bankruptcy yielding up little information, led to an order for security.⁴
7. Security from Defendant
For many years (and in many provinces?) only a plaintiff could be ordered to post security, but
in 1997 the Alberta Rule was amended, and since then it has allowed an order against any party.⁵
8. Oppression Actions
The Business Corporations Act, s.243(3) bars security for costs in an oppression action.⁶
Statutory exemption from security of a “complainant” alleging oppression must be carefully
applied and vague analogies avoided. The plaintiff should own shares in the company, or a right
to own shares.⁷
The Act means that the plaintiff must be a “complainant”. Discrete non-oppression claims,
even in the same suit, are not immune from security, e. g. a proportionate share of security for the
suit. But no security is allowed if the core of the claim is oppression. Extra claims for damages, or
contractual elements, do not attract security. One analyzes the claim; one does not merely count
the number of references to “oppression”.⁸
And where the various causes of action asserted could
not be neatly segregated and categorized, and folded into one another, though they were discrete
causes of action, the aspect of the suit which linked misrepresentation and oppression was about
35% of the action, so security was only 65% of what it otherwise would have been.⁹

4-41
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.23(1)
Contents of Security for Costs Order
4.23(1) An order to provide security for payment of a costs award must, unless
the Court otherwise orders,
(a) specify the nature of the security to be provided, which may include
payment into Court,
(b) require a party to whom the order is directed to provide the security
no later than 2 months after the date of the order or any other time
speciﬁed in the order,
(c) stay some or all applications and other proceedings in the action
until the security is provided, and
(d) state that if the security is not provided in accordance with the order,
as the case requires,
(i) all or part of an action is dismissed without further order, or
(ii) a claim or defence is struck out.¹
(2) If the security is given by bond, the bond must be given to the party requiring
security unless the Court otherwise orders.²
(3) If the security is given by money paid into Court, the money may, by agreement
of the parties, be paid out and a bond substituted for it.³
(4) As circumstances require, the Court may
(a) increase or reduce the security required to be provided, and
(b) vary the nature of the security to be provided.⁴
(5) An order under this rule may amend a complex case litigation plan.

Related Provisions
9.1 (form of orders) ; 9.2 (preparing orders) ; 14.67 (security for costs on appeal).

It is customary for an oral order for security not to spell out all the details or corollaries. This
Rule sets the default mode for those details to be put into the formal order (where nothing to the
contrary was said orally by the Applications Judge or Justice).⁵
Rule 13.5 applies to times set by
orders, and will permit extension of the time, even after it has expired, and even after the suit has
been automatically dismissed for failure to post security.
