R.4.23(5) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
Even in a family or child custody case, a court ﬁnding security for costs is ﬁtting should not
make the order unenforceable by stating in advance that failure to post the security will not remove
the rights or alter the plans of the party who is to put up the security.¹
Sometimes the security is ordered to be posted in instalments, either on ﬁxed dates, or before
certain stages in the suit.²
If the parties wish any modiﬁcation of the terms of security (such as more time to put it up),
they should apply to the trial court, even if the security has been afﬁrmed by the Court of Appeal.³
Security given by a plaintiff may be continued (held in court) after the plaintiff wins the trial, if the
defendant appeals.⁴
The court can extend the deadline previously set for giving security.⁵
There are no ﬁxed circumstances to decide when security previously ordered may be increased
or decreased.⁶
The security previously ordered may be reduced in size if in the meantime the
statement of claim has been amended to eliminate claims of a broad and amorphous nature, thus
shortening likely discovery.⁷
The court may order additional security for costs pending consideration of an ex parte pre-
judgment attachment order.⁸
On amount and type of security, see R.4.22n. F.4, supra.
The court can order almost anything as security. Cash is best, but often not available. If the
plaintiff has marketable securities or Guaranteed Investment Certiﬁcates, with appropriate custody
and assignments they should be good security. Subrule (2) contemplates bonds of indemnity, but
these are rarely tendered today. They are poor security; bonding companies tend to ﬁght claims
if their customer seems solvent. If the plaintiff is a company, at the very least it should obtain the
undertaking of the shareholders or creditors, or those really causing it to sue, that they will be
personally liable for its costs.⁹
If the company will not tender any of those things, those promoters
of the suit are just being tricky.
Bank letters of credit are often tendered,¹⁰
but are not very satisfactory, and must be carefully
negotiated and checked, because
(a) they expire fairly quickly, and one needs an elaborate mechanism for calling them if they
are not renewed;¹¹

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.24(1)
(b) they are usually the type designed for foreign trade, not as guarantees, and commonly
require sight drafts and peculiar certiﬁcates;
(c) they often must be presented in other cities at the last moment; and
(d) the bank will often ﬁnd picky excuses not to honor them, particularly if the plaintiff is the
bank’s good customer, or seems solvent, or if the bank has security from the plaintiff.¹
Security already deposited, or sums already owing by the plaintiffs, would be no security at all
and so are improper.²
If a party posted a letter of credit which is for its beneﬁt, it may have to bear the costs of getting
the letter.³
It can put up cash as security, and it chooses a letter of credit as more affordable
or cheaper.⁴
Loss of interest on money posted as security to release an arrested ship may be
awarded, if ultimately the suit against the ship fails.⁵
A party who won got back the money which she had put up as security for costs.⁶
Where an action is dismissed for failure to give the security for costs which was ordered, the
action is gone for all purposes, and cannot be revived against persons who are not the ones who
had sought the security for costs.⁷
Dismissal for want of security probably does not quite bar a
second action, but the second action will usually be stayed until all the costs of the ﬁrst action are
paid.⁸
DIVISION 5
SETTLEMENT USING COURT PROCESS
Formal Offers to Settle
4.24(1) At any time after a statement of claim, a claim under the Family Law Act,
an application to vary a custody order under the Extra provincial Enforcement of
Custody Orders Act or an originating application to vary a corollary relief order
granted by another court under the Divorce Act (Canada) is ﬁled, but 10 days or
more before
(a) a streamlined trial is scheduled to be heard, [Alta. Reg.126/23]
(b) a trial is scheduled to start, or
(c) an application is scheduled to be heard or considered,
one party may serve on the party to whom the offer is made a formal offer to
settle the action or a claim in the action.⁹

given only if the Court is satisﬁed that there are special circumstances
that justify withdrawal, and
(b) the party who made the offer serves written notice of withdrawal on
every party who received the offer. [Alta. Reg.124/10;143/11]

Footnote
9 Quite similar to previous Rr.169(1), 170(1). They were new in 1984. A number of other jurisdictions already

4-45
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.24(4)
A. Introduction
Formal offers to settle a suit are discussed.¹
Present and former formal offer processes under the present and former Alberta Rules are
mostly the same.²
But the time limits are different.³
The present 2010 Rules expressly broaden
the application of formal offers to include applications for interim orders,⁴
including orders under
the Divorce Act.
The Alberta Rules are favorable to persons who make formal offers to settle, so that it is a
mystery why any party ever fails to offer. The offer can be withdrawn after 2 months, and unless
one’s offer is unusually generous, there is little danger of a settlement on disadvantageous terms,
especially at early stages in a suit. Some of the common excuses for not making an offer do not
hold water, such as “this is an all-or-nothing lawsuit”,⁵
or “I’ll make an offer just before trial” or “I’ve
made informal offers already”, or “the remedy at trial won’t be money or damages”.
The aim of the offer Rules is to reduce claims and increase the possibility of settlement.⁶
Now
encouraging settlement is more important than ever,⁷
as the aim of the Rules of Court is to drive
early resolution.⁸
B. Who, Where, and When
An offer to settle made before the suit began cannot be a formal offer under the Rules, and so
doubling costs under R.4.29 is not mandatory. Even if it also lacks the usual formal notices, if it
is a reasonable genuine offer of compromise, with a reasonable time to consider it, it can lead to
some enhancement of Schedule C costs.⁹
There is no set stage for an offer to settle, and an offer can be good and have later costs
effects, even if made before discoveries.¹⁰
Rule 4.24 allows any party to a lawsuit to make an ofﬁcial offer to settle.
An unaccepted offer could not affect costs where merely accepting the offer would not have
settled the suit; the municipality would have to be a party to the settlement.¹¹
For purposes of these Rules, a third party defendant is treated like a defendant and can make
an offer.¹²

R.4.24(4) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
An offer under this Rule binds a lawyer’s clients, even if he or she has misunderstood the
client’s instructions, or made an error in drafting.¹
These Rules also apply to proceedings before the Court of Appeal.²
But to affect costs of the
appeal, an offer must be made or renewed pending appeal.³
Where an offer was served after an
appeal commenced, but was headed in Queen's Bench, it was held to be an attempt only to settle
Queen's Bench costs.⁴
Matrimonial litigation follows the usual principles for offers and costs in any
civil cases.⁵
Even a judicial review (administrative law) matter may be settled.⁶
An offer may be to
compromise an appeal from an Applications Judge to a justice.⁷
An application for an injunction was met by an offer to settle by accepting an abandonment of
that application with no costs. As the offer was made after pleadings and afﬁdavits had been ﬁled
and afﬁdavits cross-examined on, that was a genuine offer of compromise. As the motion for an
injunction later failed, this offer should have been accepted.⁸
C. What is a Proper Offer
1. Cross-Reference
On whether an offer must involve an element of compromise, or be fair to the offeree, or be
reasonable, see R.4.29 n. B.
The offer-to-settle procedure is not conﬁned to lump sums, nor indeed to money at all. The
offer can be creative, and tailored to the facts of the case.⁹
An offer to settle an interlocutory application, or to settle an appeal of that, can be a valid
formal offer; trying to settle the whole lawsuit is not necessary.¹⁰
Many lawyers do not seem to have
thought about that, though an interlocutory application can be slow and expensive today.
2. Informal Offers
An offer saying that it is made pursuant to the Rules is enough to engage Rule 4.24,¹¹
whereas
one not mentioning Rules and saying that it is without prejudice except as to costs, is a mere

Calderbank letter.¹
An offer has no mandatory force under the Rules, unless it is plain that it is
made under, or will invoke, the Rules.²
But in 2020 R.10.33(2) was enacted, and its paragraph (b)
says any offer of settlement must be considered in assessing costs, even an offer not complying
with Part 4 Division 5, including an informal “Calderbank” offer.
The 2010 Rule requires that the offer cite costs consequences of not accepting.³
An offer headed “without prejudice” is valid under the Rules, absent special circumstances,
and absent words showing that the settlement privilege alluded to will extend beyond the usual
time (end of the substantive dispute).⁴
English courts have long given some effect to informal offers to settle, often called “Calderbank
letters”, though some Canadian provinces often are unreceptive. In Alberta and the Northwest
Territories, the courts are not bound to give such an informal offer full costs effect,⁵
but have a
discretion to give it effect, and must give it some weight.⁶
If the letter does not say whether or not
it is privileged, and says nothing about any effect on costs, nor about telling the court about it
later, probably the court need not give it any effect in setting costs.⁷
If it says that it can be referred
to for costs purposes, that is proper,⁸

even held to supersede an earlier formal offer.¹
Courts should not try to discourage such informal
Calderbank offers.²
An offer not complying with the Rules (e. g. too late) should not get stronger
effect than one complying with the Rules.³
An offer in good faith not quite complying with the
Rules, should get some consideration re costs.⁴
Using an informal offer to double costs is especially good where the length and scope of trial
and argument were disproportionate to the amount in issue, and it was a builder’s lien suit, and
the defendant was slow with an afﬁdavit of records and tried to dodge its own admissions.⁵
The Rules do not prohibit or restrict offers outside their scope.⁶
They can have costs
consequences if they are with prejudice, or are without prejudice except as to costs.⁷
The wording
of the letter is not important; the substance is, such as a clear offer to settle, open for a reasonable
time, and cheaper than the result at trial.⁸
The court can always make an unreasonable party pay
costs, and failure to accept a reasonable offer may sufﬁce for that purpose.⁹
An offer need not be signed, and in any event, lack of a signature does not invalidate it.¹⁰
3. Certainty
An offer can be void for uncertainty (possibly because if accepted, the resulting agreement
would be void for uncertainty). So an offer is bad if it requires signing a release whose wording is
not provided, and is to be agreed on later by both parties.¹¹
4. Conditional or Complex Offers
Offers which reserve the question of liability of someone else to the plaintiff can be complicated.¹²
A global offer by the plaintiff to all defendants is valid.¹³
An offer which has to be accepted by every
defendant is valid, so long as no defendant has to do more than accept.¹⁴
An offer which the offeree

cannot simply accept, and is conditional upon a matter beyond the offeree’s control, is not a
proper offer under these Rules.¹
Similarly, all the offerees must accept an offer which is addressed
to all, or which offers to compromise the whole suit.²
That an offer to settle is addressed to two
parties is no ﬂaw, if one of them can independently accept and perform with no obligation by the
other to approve. That each offeree has to accept does not invalidate it.³
Unless clearly stated otherwise, an offer is presumed to be a single linked package which can
be accepted in full or not at all.⁴
A formal offer not broken down between heads of damages can still have costs effects.⁵
An offer must be to settle the whole claim, not just pecuniary heads of damage?⁶
5. Costs and Interest in Offer
An offer of “$ x plus interest plus costs” is presumed to mean costs and interest up to the date
of service of the offer, the costs on the column appropriate for $x.⁷
A defendant offered to settle
for a stated lump sum “plus taxable costs and pre-judgment interest”. The offer was purportedly
accepted two years after. The taxable costs and interest should only be calculated up to the date
when the offer was made (and if it is renewed, then up to that date). A later letter saying when the
offer would expire is irrelevant, and does not keep interest or costs running.⁸
An offer of judgment by the defendant of $x (plus interest and costs) is not ambiguous to
anyone, even if the plaintiff has earlier got no-fault beneﬁts for which the defendant by law always
gets credit. Had the plaintiff accepted, he would have got all the money offered.⁹
6. Miscellaneous
There is nothing the matter with an offer to settle two parallel and closely related appeals (one
over the merits and one over the trial costs).¹⁰
But an offer is not “real” if made at an early stage
and offering to resolve a complex big suit in chambers?¹¹
Statutory offers to buy shares, required in dissenting shareholder proceedings, can also be
looked at for costs.¹²

R.4.24(4) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
The 2010 Rule prevents offers being made less than 10 days before trial.¹
A contract that one side would pay all the other’s “legal fees and expenses…regardless of the
outcome” of the suit, excluded the Rules on formal offers.²
D. Duration of Offer
Under the previous Rules, the court could retroactively bless a party’s withdrawal of an offer
before the usual expiry, on proof of wilful and material nondisclosure by the opponent offeree
during discoveries.³
A small error in an offer does not invalidate it.⁴
Is withdrawal governed solely by the Rules and not the law of contracts?⁵
If an offer says that
it expires after a certain number of days, and it is orally withdrawn after that, it cannot later be
accepted even if the offeree had not actually got it, if it went to an address for service.⁶
An offer
may be revoked at 4: 30 p. m. on the last day of the two-month period?⁷
Rejecting a recent offer to settle impliedly revokes a previous formal offer in the other direction
to settle for the same sum. If a formal offer is accepted, the result is much like a release, so
mention in only one offer of a release is not a signiﬁcant difference.⁸
A later formal offer to settle does not rescind an earlier formal offer. Offers expire only in
accordance with their terms. Because double costs are “punitive”, the offers must be certain and
exact, to be given effect.⁹
An offer may say that it will expire at a certain point, and so long as that is at least 2 months
away, that is effective.¹⁰
An offer need not be outstanding for 2 months to be valid, if it is given less
than 2 months before the start of trial.¹¹

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.24(4)
Offers too late, a few days before trial, and not open long enough, were not valid and did not
affect costs.¹
Such offers do not encourage settlement.²
E. Miscellaneous
In principle a costs argument arising after November 1, 2010 should be heard under the 2010
Rules, but an offer made before then and valid then, cannot be invalidated by the 2010 Rules.³
An offer to settle all claims in an action means all, not just the one still outstanding (others
having been earlier settled).⁴
Can an offer be sent directly to the opposing party, rather than through his solicitor?⁵
Service
by email sufﬁces, if the offeree actually gets the offer or is aware of it.⁶
Formal offers can be orally amended by consent of both offeror and offeree, especially to
cure an obvious clerical error. Once amended, they can be accepted as amended. Continuous
agreement is not necessary; it is only necessary that the offer accepted not have been withdrawn.⁷
Accepting an offer ends the suit, and so it is presumed that the court cannot award costs for
steps after settlement (if it was retroactive).⁸
CHECKLIST FOR OFFER TO SETTLE
A. Timing
1. Will offeror’s position get a lot better in next 2 months?
2. Offer cannot be made less than 10 days before trial (R.4.24(1) )

3 Are there 2 months left until beginning of trial? If less than 2 months, offer cannot be
accepted after trial commences or start of hearing of application for judgment (R.4.24(3) ).
4. Are any interlocutory steps with heavy costs (e. g. for experts) coming up?
5. Diarize time to revoke offer (2 months) (R.4.24(3) ).
6. New offer would be needed if appeal is ﬁled.
7. Should offer contain an expiry date? (See #4 above)
B. Amount
1. Avoid a round number; go a bit higher (if you are defendant) or a bit lower (if you are
plaintiff).
2. Include enough to allow for special damages.
3. It is often safe to offer generously at an early stage.
4. (a) There is little downside to a cheap offer (i. e. generous to the offeror). One can always
make a more generous one later,
(b) provided always that the offer is a genuine offer of compromise, and not a call for
surrender or close to it.

C. Other Contents
1. Separate offer for each plaintiff?
2. Is the offer clear and unambiguous? Courts have criticized these aspects in some cases
where it is not clear whether:
(a) offer is to settle all the claims, or only part, and which it is presumed that all parts are
linked and must all be accepted?
(b) under Part 4 (or under common law) : cite Part 4 speciﬁcally?
(c) which party or parties are offering?
(d) the wording or form of a later act is to be one thing or another (void for uncertainty).
3. How to handle costs in the offer? Recall the default mode: the plaintiff accepting gets
costs up to date of offer. Plaintiff’s offer adds costs to acceptance unless offer says
otherwise.
4. Offer to surrender any counterclaim?
5. Ensure offer contains all that Form 22 and R.4.24(2) require.

1. Rules are not applicable if defence of tender is pleaded; see R.4.30 instead.
2. Consider a Calderbank letter, if Rules seem too restrictive?
3. First get authority from client in writing. Any disability?
4. Serve offer.
5. Don’t refer to offer publicly (with exceptions) (R.4.28).

Acceptance of Formal Offer to Settle
4.25(1) For the purpose of this Division, a formal offer to settle an action or a
claim in an action may only be accepted in accordance with this rule.
(2) At any time a formal offer to settle remains open for acceptance or before a
formal offer to settle is withdrawn, a party to whom the offer has been made may
accept the offer by
(a) ﬁling the offer and the acceptance of it, and
(b) serving on the party who made the offer notice that
(i) the offer has been accepted, and
(ii) the terms of any judgment or order in the offer have been
agreed to.
(3) After the ﬁling and service, a party may
(a) apply to the Court for judgment or an order in accordance with the
terms of the formal offer to settle,
(b) continue the action in respect of any matter not covered by the
judgment or order, and

4-53
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.26
(c) continue the action against any party who is not a party to the
settlement.¹

Related Provisions
3.35 (approving consent orders)

Acceptance of formal offers is governed by the Rules, not by common-law contracts authorities.
If there was no improper failure to disclose, there was no proper ground to let the offeror withdraw
the offer.²
Some offers can be accepted by words of acceptance, while others require acts such as
paying money, to accept them. Most offers, if accepted, will result in a consent judgment. The
result depends on the precise terms of the offer. In order to accept a plaintiff’s formal offer of
judgment, the defendant has to ﬁle a copy of the offer, a copy of his acceptance, and either the
sums speciﬁed in the offer or a confession of judgment (depending on what the offer proposes).
He also has to tell the plaintiff of his acceptance and payment in. It takes all these things to accept.³
If the offer is accepted, it must be accepted unconditionally. If the terms of the offer allow that,
one offeree alone can accept it.⁴
If an offer or its covering letter requires acceptance by 2 or more
people, one alone cannot accept, even if it is about his claim only.⁵
Settlement for “$x plus costs to be taxed” does not affect costs already payable because of
past interlocutory motions.⁶
If one party tries to accept an offer while the offeror is trying to withdraw it, the acceptance
needs to be strictly correct; otherwise the offeror can withdraw the offer.⁷
Under the former Rules, the defendant paid money into court for a proposed settlement. It was
not accepted and was then forgotten. After over 5 years of inaction, and a stay, the plaintiff was
able to accept the offer and take out the money.⁸
If Costs are Not Dealt With in Formal Offer to Settle
4.26 If a formal offer to settle and acceptance ﬁled under rule 4.25 [Acceptance
of formal offer to settle] does not deal with costs, either party may apply to the
Court for an order under rule 10.31 [Court ordered costs award].

10.31 (costs award)

The party accepting the offer is entitled to payment of his costs accrued to that point.¹
That is
important, and often overlooked or not understood. If it is some years since the offer, which has
never been revoked or expired, that can be a large sum of additional costs.
The offer Rules are framed to encourage early settlement.²

4.27 Unless otherwise agreed by the parties, a formal offer to settle under this
Division
(a) is to be considered as an offer to settle that is made without prejudice,
and
(b) is not an admission of anything.³

Conﬁdentiality of Formal Offer to Settle
4.28(1) Subject to rule 4.24(4) [Formal offers to settle] and subrule (2), a formal
offer to settle is to be kept conﬁdential and not disclosed to the Court until
(a) it is accepted, or
(b) the remedy for the claim has been decided.
(2) Subrule (1) does not apply to an offer of a written or printed apology made
under section 4 of the Defamation Act.⁵

4-55
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.29(1)
Formal offers are without prejudice¹
(unless the offeror states otherwise. ) They admit nothing.
Therefore, the offer must be kept secret from any jury, and the trial judge or Court of Appeal must
not be told until judgment is pronounced. Improperly revealing the offer might lead to a new trial
or other relief.²
On the duration of the privilege, see Mahe case.³
At the end of the suit once liability and
quantum are decided, then the court can look at an offer to settle for costs purposes.⁴
It is done
all the time.
Neither a chambers justice nor the Court of Appeal can waive this Rule, so a ground of appeal
complaining of failure to waive the Rule, is hopeless.⁵
Admission into evidence of a formal offer to
settle was reversed on appeal as a violation of this Rule, and revealing parties’ intent, and waiver
by one party only, are no exception to this important rule of privilege.⁶
But a concluded agreement to settle among some (not all) of the parties must be notiﬁed to
the court.⁷
Costs Consequences of Formal Offer to Settle
4.29(1) Subject to subrule (4), if a plaintiff makes a formal offer to settle that is
not accepted and subsequently obtains a judgment or order in the action that is
equal to or more favourable to the plaintiff than the offer, the plaintiff is entitled
to double the costs to which the plaintiff would otherwise have been entitled
under rule 10.31(1) (a) [Court-ordered costs award] or 10.32 [Costs in a class
proceeding] for all steps taken in relation to the action or claim after service of
the offer, excluding disbursements.⁸
(2) Subject to subrule (4), if a defendant makes a formal offer to settle that is not
accepted and a judgment or order in the action is made that is equal to or more
favourable to the defendant than the offer, the defendant is entitled to costs for
all steps taken in the action in relation to the action or the claim after service of
the offer.⁹

Related Provisions
10.31(6) (general discretion over costs)

A. Introduction
The purpose of the formal offer Rules is to encourage parties to compromise early, and
discourage turning down a chance to do so or gambling.²
These costs consequences are the
essential consequence (mechanism) of the formal offer Rules.³
The costs consequences are part
of the intent of the Rules to encourage always the quickest means including settlement, as R.1.2
says expressly.⁴
Formal offers to settle are important, and would become meaningless if courts
declined to apply the costs consequences intended to ﬂow from successful offers.⁵
Rule 4.29 prevails over another costs Rule (e. g. on who pays costs to have live expert evidence
at trial).⁶

Footnote
1 Quite similar to previous R.174(1.1). It was ﬁrst enacted by Alta. Reg.152/98.
2 Yaworski v. Alliance Pipeline (2001) 291 AR 184; Mostafa Altalibi Pro. Corp. v. Lorne S. Kamelchuk
Pro. Corp.2022 ABCA 364, 56 Alta LR(7th) 308 (¶’s 9-12) ; H2S v. Tourmaline, infra; Avli BRC Devs. v.
BMP Constr. Mgmt. (#2) 2023 ABCA 267, Calg 2201 0003 AC (Sep 23) (¶’s 4-6). Therefore cases from
other provinces ignoring offers and their costs consequences because the offeror’s motive was to get costs,
fundamentally misunderstand the whole process. No litigant is required to be altruistic, still less a charity, and
the Rules deliberately impose sticks and award carrots, in order to modify behaviour.
3 Mostafa Altalibi v. Kamelchuk, supra. Which makes very surprising any suggestion that the offeror should
not be motivated by costs considerations.
4 Mostafa Altalibi v. Kamelchuk, supra.
5 Pillar Res. Serv. v. Prime W. Enr. (#2) 2017 ABCA 141, 51 Alta LR(6th) 65 (¶ 15) ; Baim v. N. Country
Catering (#2) 2017 ABCA 332, 2017 CarswellAlta 812.
6 Petz v. Duguay 2018 ABCA 402, Calg 1701 0064 AC (Nov 29) (¶ 34), leave den (SCC 13 Jun ’19).

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.29(4)
What is a proper, valid offer is dealt with in the notes to Rr.4.24, 4.25, supra. Whether an offer
is accepted or not, and the rules for that, are discussed in R.4.25n. above.
B. Nominal Offers
See blogs¹
in Legalviews.
1. Time to Test
The basic scheme of these costs under those Rules (and their predecessors) is that one looks
at the result of the lawsuit after the fact, not at what was easy to foretell at the outset; the proof
of the pudding is in the eating.²
But costs which the offeror incurs between serving the offer and
winning the suit or application later, can be looked at to see if an offer to waive such costs was a
genuine offer of compromise.³
The Rules about formal offers are designed to reinforce the public policy which encourages
parties to settle their lawsuit by agreement.⁴
2. Nil or Tiny Concession
The cases formerly conﬂicted on whether the offer can be for the exact amount of the offeror’s
claim.⁵
The fact that an offer to settle by the plaintiff purportedly made under the Rules is for
the full amount and does not knock a penny off, or that an offer by the defendant is that the
plaintiff discontinue without costs, means that the offer does not compel a costs result.⁶
Now a
genuine offer of compromise is a necessary condition for double costs.⁷
An offer by the creditor
(respondent on appeal) offering to knock $1 off the amount of maintenance (previously ordered)
is not a genuine offer of compromise meeting these Rules.⁸
Where the amount of damages was

R.4.29(4) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
agreed at $725 thousand so only liability was in dispute, an informal offer to settle for $700
thousand (3.5% less) was held to be a no-risk strategy and not a genuine offer.¹
A dictum in one case says that an important or key circumstance for genuineness, is whether
there was enough information to make an informed offer.²
The onus of proof is on the party asserting that an offer to him was not a genuine offer of
compromise.³
3. Costs Waiver or Other Concession
When evaluating an offer under R.4.29, one must take into account future costs, not just those
already ordered.⁴
Sometimes an offer to take a discontinuance without costs is not a genuine offer of compromise
satisfying the Rules either.⁵
For example, if the costs to that point are very small,⁶
or non-existent.⁷
But an offer to waive costs to which one is entitled is good.⁸
Therefore, one cannot assess whether
there is a genuine offer of compromise merely from the face of the offer. If accepting it at once
would deny the offeror signiﬁcant costs already accrued to that point, then it is a genuine offer of
compromise.⁹
[Footnote 8 continued from prior page]
All surrounding circumstances, objective and subjective, must be looked at, including the relative meritsof
the parties’ positions at the time, and whether there is no reasonable expectation that the offer will be
accepted: Allen v. Mueller (Univ. Hosp. Bd. ) (#2) 2006 ABCA 101, 384 AR 23. An offer was a genuine offer
because there was some that hope the plaintiffs would accept: Tonizzo v. Moysa (#2) 2007 ABQB 379. A
dictum that in some circumstances an offer to knock off $6, 500 might not be a genuine offer of compromise
(in the Union Sq. Apts. case, supra, ¶ 21) sounds very dubious.

offer to pay a nominal sum is a genuine compromise because of all the costs to date which it would reverse:
Fullowka v. Royal Oak Ventures (#2) 2008 NWTCA 9, 437 AR 390, affd 2010 SCC 5.

4-59
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.29(4)
And if the offer would drop costs, one looks at both the costs accrued to date and the costs
which would be incurred in future by going on with the suit and not accepting the offer (and which
were later incurred).¹
The crucial thing is to see what costs would (likely) be incurred up to the
expiry date of the offer to waive costs.²
The issue is not the ratio of the offer to the total amount in
issue; hundreds of thousands of dollars are never nominal.³
An offer is genuine even if its offer to
waive costs does not involve huge sums.⁴
Where a suit was an all-or-nothing proposition, and an
appeal had little chance of success, an offer to settle the appeal, by the respondent, and offering
to waive costs, was held to have been a genuine offer of compromise.⁵
Certainly an offer to settle
for $1.00 plus dropping a cross claim or appeal, is genuine and effective.⁶
One cannot call an
offer to drop a cross-claim worthless because later it failed at trial; the test of a concession is not
hindsight. A large claim with a small chance of success is worth something.⁷
If an offer to settle impliedly offers to drop the offeror’s accrued costs or his cross-appeal or
similar proceeding, that is a clear offer of compromise, and there is no argument against applying
R.4.29 on costs.⁸
Some criteria whether an offer is “genuine” are cited.⁹
The fact that an offer was made early in
the suit does not suggest that it was not a genuine offer of compromise.¹⁰
The compromise offered in the formal offer need not be an adjustment up or down of the debt
or damages claimed, nor dropping costs; it can be removing some other signiﬁcant practical cost
[Footnote 9 from prior page]

Footnote
9 Mraz v. Herman (#2) 2016 ABQB 14, 2016 CarswellAlta 14 (Jan 8) ; Fletcher v. Davidson & Williams 2015
ABQB 783, JCL 1406 00800 (Dec 9) ; Baim v. N. Country Catering (#2) 2017 ABCA 332, 2017 CarswellAlta
812; Carroll v. Atco Elec. (#__) 2018 ABCA 186, [2018] 7 WWR 469; H2S Solutions v. Tourmaline, supra.
Double costs were given because of a formal offer to take a discontinuance without costs, which offer was
not accepted, though the offer was made at outset of suit: 1529771 Alta. v. John Bosco Child etc. (M)
2016 ABQB 378, JCE 1503 14266 (Jul 6). A formal offer of settlement which includes an offer to permit a
discontinuance of the opponent’s suit or appeal “on a without costs basis” is also in effect waiving any costs
which the offeror might obtain if it succeeded in the appeal (suit), as part of that offer: Impact Painting v.
Man-Shield (Alta. ) Constr. (#2) 2019 ABCA 213, Edm 1803 0003 AC (May 28) (¶ 3).

R.4.29(4) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
on either or both sides. Clearly walking away from likely (but not certain) success without costs
is a genuine compromise in the offer.¹
And new information such as a new recent evaluation, or
a new payment, may make an offer a real compromise, even if earlier that same offer probably
would not have been one.²
The onus of proof lies on the party who asserts that the opponent’s proposal was not a genuine
offer of compromise (or was ill-informed, unreasonable, or tactical only).³
4. Reasonableness is Not the Test
The Rules on formal offers do not require a signiﬁcant concession for an offer to be effective;
previous Court of Appeal decisions on this and similar topics such as “reasonable” or “genuine”
offers, are carefully analyzed.⁴
Especially where the party offering has a strong liquidated case.
Sometimes it is reasonable to make an offer early in a simple case with known documents. And
as to compromise, one also looks to interest being waived, not just the amount of principal to be
knocked off.⁵
How the offeror calculated or decided on its offer, or what subcomponents it had in mind, are
irrelevant.⁶
The Court of Appeal has overruled the idea that the offer, or the response to it, must
be “reasonable”.⁷
Some questionable recent King's Bench decisions had overlooked or misstated
past authorities, and required that an offer affecting costs have realistically reﬂected the relative
merit of the parties’ positions at the time, and even have been made with a realistic expectation
that the offeree would accept it. The H2S decision now overrules that.⁸
An admission during the lawsuit may remove part or all of the dispute, making an offer to settle
effective to after costs.⁹

4-61
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.29(4)
C. Costs Effects
Rule 4.29 is compulsory unless “special circumstances” are shown and found.¹
That a suit is all or nothing is irrelevant, and no reason to bypass the costs effects of an offer.²
Where the offer and the ﬁnal judgment are identical, who offered (plaintiff or defendant) makes
a difference, and in the case of exact equality like that, the advantage is with the offeror.³
This Rule gives the costs consequences of not accepting an offer by the opposing party. If the
offeree does not accept and does not get more success at trial than the opponent’s offer, there
is an effect on costs. The effect is only on costs from the time of service of the offer or notice of
payment in.⁴
If a plaintiff does not get a judgment at trial better than the defendant’s offer, that plaintiff
will later be ordered to pay costs from the date of that offer or payment rather than receiving
them.⁵
This often effectively doubles the defendant’s costs, because if there had been no offer,
a defendant usually would have had to pay costs to a plaintiff who recovers any amount. And if
the defendant is totally successful,⁶
subrule (3) calls for double costs for the defendant.⁷
If the
plaintiff recovers at trial less than the defendant’s formal offer, but the Court of Appeal increases
the plaintiff’s award to more than that original offer, the plaintiff gets full costs of the trial.⁸
One case gave the plaintiff double costs for bettering the defendant’s offer to settle.⁹
On partially bettering an offer, see the McNulty case, infra, and Part E generally.
An offer of settlement unaccepted is relevant only to the conduct of the offeree, as others
cannot accept it.¹⁰
An offer made before suit brought cannot be a formal offer and so cannot make double costs
mandatory, but not bettering it can lead to higher costs than usual.¹¹

An informal offer of settlement which was not bettered led to double the fee portion of the costs
(here twice Schedule C, Column 5).¹
Informal "Calderbank" offers are relevant to costs, but not
mandatory.²
D. Double Costs
See also Part C above.
Rule 4.29 mandates double costs when a defendant makes an offer which is not accepted,
and then wins the suit entirely,³
unless the plaintiff shows special reason.⁴
An offer yields double
costs only if it is bettered in its entirety.⁵
“Close” counts only in a game of horseshoes.⁶
But an
offeror respondent who almost entirely wins, but not quite, is not entitled as of right to double
costs, though the court can give him more than ordinary costs; but where his opponent offeree got
signiﬁcant relief, that should not be done either.⁷
See also Part E.2 below.
At trial, if a defendant does not better the plaintiff’s offer, the plaintiff’s party-party costs fees
become double the usual amount.⁸
The same is true of a defendant’s offer if the suit fails entirely.⁹
“Double costs” refer only to the fee part of costs, not to disbursements. A later lump-sum costs
award can be doubled because of an earlier formal order; the Rule is not conﬁned to Schedule
C costs.¹⁰
If the size and complexity of the case and the misconduct of the loser would lead to a multiple
of Schedule C, beating an offer may produce double that multiple!¹¹
However, if the award before
considering R.4.29 is solicitor-client costs, those are not usually doubled.¹²
And if costs are doubled
under R.4.29, they should not be doubled again (quadrupled) because there is a counterclaim.¹³
Nor should the costs of the costs hearing be doubled?¹⁴
But second counsel fees may be doubled.¹⁵

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.29(4)
A penalty for pleading fraud may be doubled.¹
Since R.4.29 has aims beyond indemnity, some
cases held that double fees were called for even when they exceeded the winner’s actual legal
bills,²
but that seems overruled now.³
If costs are split because of mixed success on different issues, the fraction which one party
gets may be doubled because of an offer.⁴
If the plaintiff betters its offer it usually gets double
costs, but if results are divided, the Court of Appeal may offset the two considerations, and give
the plaintiff a single set of costs.⁵
Doubled costs can properly exceed the damages awarded.⁶
Where an appellant receives an offer to settle for the trial judgment, from the respondent, after
the trial judgment and before the argument of the appeal, then that Rule applies.⁷
Double costs
of appeals were given in a number of cases.⁸
An appeal having failed entirely, the respondent got
double costs of it.⁹
An offer to settle before trial is not relevant on appeal if it has not been renewed
in the meantime.¹⁰
Costs already ordered by a previous judge cannot be doubled because of later bettering an
offer.¹¹
E. Bettering an Offer
1. Onus of Proof
The party relying on this Rule has the onus of proving that he or she did better at trial than the
offer.¹²
The result at trial or on appeal must be clearly poorer than the offer; if the offer had many

conditions, that may not be so.¹
If the Court of Appeal could not tell which was bigger, it may send
that question back to the trial judge to decide.²
Which party was successful depends on a balance
of all the issues, a global approach.³
Whether a party bettered an offer requires bettering on a net
basis overall (which may require some arithmetic).⁴
If most elements are approximately a wash,
then a cash payment and costs awarded means the recovery was greater than the offer.⁵
The Rule is not conﬁned to money offers.⁶
Costs consequences can come from an earlier
offer, even if one issue in the suit involves adoption or custody or other right which cannot be given
a money value.⁷
If an offer calls for something which the court cannot order (such as a release),
then no judgment can ever be said to beat the offer?⁸
2. Exact Numbers
Close is not good enough to affect costs: the offer must be over to be relevant, or at least to
bind the court.⁹
3. Multiple Offerees
Where the trial judgment found all the defendants liable, but in varying percentages, and the
plaintiff’s total offer was for less money, and one defendant did not accept the plaintiff’s offer,
which had to be accepted by all defendants, the plaintiff bettered his offer, and the non-accepting
offeree had to pay all the plaintiff’s costs after date of the offer.¹⁰

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.29(4)
What one defendant’s share of damages after trial would be is irrelevant, if the damages are
payable jointly and severally.¹
4. Questions No Longer Disputed
For purposes of R.4.29, a party does not “obtain” an order or judgment for an amount which
his opponent concedes all along and shows as a deduction (credit) in its pleading.²
Nor can the court weigh intangible merits or difﬁculties between the offer and the judgment.³
Even though ultimately the defendant betters his earlier all-inclusive offer, he does not get
double costs if in the meantime the parties settled important elements of the suit, leaving a small
fraction to try?⁴
In a trafﬁc accident suit, one looks at section B beneﬁts too.⁵
One also looks at all claims which
the offer impliedly required be discontinued.⁶
5. Collateral Results
In assessing whether a party bettered a formal offer, for costs purposes, one looks at the face
of the judgment and offer, and does not try to calculate income tax effects, even in a wrongful
dismissal suit.⁷
In maintenance cases, one sees whether the offer and the award are before or
after taxes.⁸
6. Interest
See also subpart 7 below.
To see whether the plaintiff did better at trial than an offer or payment in,⁹
one includes interest
up to the date of offer (but not beyond)¹⁰
in her trial recovery, but not costs. That is partly because

the Rules say that when an offer is accepted, either party can apply for a costs award. And any
costs award made in ignorance of the offer is in a sense tentative anyway, and so to rely on the
costs award so made as taking the trial recovery over the amount of the offer, would be to use
circular reasoning.¹
To see if the plaintiff beat the defendant’s offer, one ignores extra costs which
the plaintiff may get for beating his own offer.²
7. Appeals
Where there is an offer made at trial which is not quite exceeded by the offeror, but then on
appeal the interest is changed by the Court of Appeal so that the ﬁnal judgment does exceed the
offer, the costs in the trial court are doubled.³
Might the defendant ever be treated like a plaintiff under these Rules, if it is appellant and the
offer is made after the appeal is ﬁled?⁴
But now note R.14.59(4).
Where a reference to ﬁx damages is ordered on appeal, the costs may be conditional on
whether the damages exceed the amount paid in by the defendant.⁵
But an offer before trial,
never renewed, may not apply to costs after a far later proceeding, such as a partial retrial after
an appeal, especially if the Court of Appeal in the meantime ﬁxed costs in the trial court.⁶
Extra costs for beating an offer pending appeal should be explored at the appeal hearing, but
nevertheless may be dealt with later.⁷
F. Exceptions to Extra Costs
1. History
At one time, trial judges would often ﬁnd excuses to give a plaintiff his or her costs even
though the defendant had paid into court a little more that the plaintiff got at trial.⁸
But after the

Footnote
1984 general amendments to the Rules, Alberta courts can no longer evade the Rule and so
give an offer no costs effects. Their wording makes these costs effects mandatory.⁹

3. Multiple Offerees
If an offer to settle by the plaintiff was intended to be accepted by all the defendants, and
was not to be effective if accepted by one defendant only, the plaintiff’s victory at trial over one
defendant only did not therefore constitute beating its offer, and the offer had no costs effects.⁵
When computing costs, the court should ignore an offer which was not accepted, and was not
capable of being independently accepted by the party to whom it was addressed and from whom
elevated costs are sought. The offer was carefully drafted and plainly required the acceptance or
agreement of other parties as well.⁶
Special reason is the test only against the defendant recovering costs, not against the co-
defendant paying them, but on a motion for a Sanderson order, it may be relevant that the
defendant ultimately paying costs did not know of the plaintiff’s offer and could do nothing to have
it accepted.⁷
A successful defendant entitled to double costs against the plaintiff because of an offer can
pass, those double costs on through a Sanderson order to the unsuccessful defendants, even
though they did not have an opportunity to accept the offer.⁸
G. Miscellaneous
What look like very large party-party costs, especially after doubling, may not in fact be so
large if one recalls the large amount for which the plaintiff sued.⁹
Rule 4.29 does not affect costs incurred at or before the time the offer was made, e. g. costs of
a pleading served along with the formal offer.¹⁰
Custody and access disputes at one time did not ﬁt well within these Rules.¹¹
Whether or not the offeree receiving a formal offer to settle an appeal or suit had counsel at
the time of the offer is not relevant to the validity or effect of the offer.¹²

4-70
R.4.30 PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
Rule 4.29 also applies to costs which the plaintiff has to pay to third parties¹
and to costs
payable by a plaintiff whose suit is automatically dismissed for failure to post security for costs.²
A Master interpreted R.4.29 as being limited to justices, and said that she had no power to
award increased costs under it, and a motion for that would have to be made to a justice.³
Is that
limited to cases where there has been a trial, or judgment ordered by a judge? But R.4.29 is
appropriate for appeals from the Alberta Court of Justice to the Court of King's Bench.⁴
In any
event, current R.4.29 does not say “judge” and an Applications Judge is part of the Court, and so
the Rule should apply to Applications Judges too.
An assessment ofﬁcer (deciding fees payable by a client to his lawyer) can award double
costs, under Rr.4.29 and 10.41(3) (d).⁵
Failure to accept an offer is no ground for solicitor-client costs on an application, if acceptance
of the offer would not have obviated, and did not necessitate, this application, because the offer
was to settle a different motion not before the court this time.⁶
An offer to settle the whole suit had
no effect on costs of an application about only one aspect of the suit.⁷
Where R.4.29 produces double costs, interest on them begins to run from the underlying
substantive judgment, even before their precise amount is quantiﬁed.⁸
An offer not complying with or made under the Rules, but expressly referring to costs
consequences, may later be looked at in awarding costs.⁹
See also R.4.24 n. C.2 above.
The policy pulls in both directions where a self-represented litigant does better than his formal
offer. In a situation where he would have got double costs (double fees) if he had been represented
by a lawyer, he got one-third the normal fees from the time of the offer.¹⁰
If a plaintiff orders expensive expert reports (in a personal injury lawsuit) in the face of an
existing offer from the defendant, that does not bar including the reasonable cost of the reports in
costs from the defendant.¹¹
When This Division Does Not Apply
4.30 This Division does not apply to an action or a claim in an action in respect
of which a defence of tender before the action started is pleaded unless that
defence is ﬁrst withdrawn.¹²

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.31(1)
Information Note
Rule 13.9 [Defence of tender] sets out the conditions for a defence of tender before action.

Related Provisions
3.9 (defence of tender).

Rule 13.9 sets the procedure for that defence.¹
DIVISION 6
DELAY IN AN ACTION
Application to Deal With Delay
4.31(1) If delay occurs in an action, on application the Court may
(a) dismiss all or any part of a claim if the Court determines that the
delay has resulted in signiﬁcant prejudice to a party, or
(b) make a procedural order or any other order provided for by these
rules.²
(2) Where, in determining an application under this rule, the Court ﬁnds that the
delay in an action is inordinate and inexcusable, that delay is presumed to have
resulted in signiﬁcant prejudice to the party that brought the application.
(3) In determining whether to dismiss all or any part of a claim under this rule, or
whether the delay is inordinate or inexcusable, the Court must consider whether
the party that brought the application participated in or contributed to the delay.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;140/13;72/22]

Related Provisions
4.1 (responsibilities of the parties in litigation) ; 4.4(2) (proposal as to timing of action) ; 5.1(1)

(e) (discouraging delay due to discovery) ; 13.5 (variation of time) ; 14.62 (delay on appeals).
A. References
Part 4, Division 6 was heavily rewritten in 2010, and prejudice is presumed from inordinate
delay again in the 2013 amendment. But cases under the 1968 Rules and their amendments are
still helpful³
in illustrating the type of prejudice that will lead to an action’s being struck out.

1944 C. R.294. They came from Eng. O.36 r.12 (am. July 1903 and 1937 #3), and from Sask. R.201.

R.4.31(3) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
There are useful textbooks.¹
On denying prejudgment interest for the plaintiff’s delay in getting to trial, see R.3.39n.
B. Importance and General Test
Rule 4.33 is about a gap in things done by the plaintiff, whereas R.4.31 is about overall delay,
looking at the entire history and progress of the suit.²
At one time, justices and masters used to be very reluctant to use R.4.31’s predecessors,
often relying on irrelevant circumstances to excuse doing nothing.³
That mistaken approach has
become much less common now, and the Court of Appeal keeps increasing the urgency and
importance of this Rule.⁴
The harm done to everyone by delay in litigation is stressed.⁵
The courts’ resources are better
devoted to other suits, not the ones neglected by those with the duty to prosecute.⁶
Oddly, Canadian courts have been willing to dismiss a criminal prosecution for some months’
delay, but sometimes reluctant to dismiss a civil suit for over a decade’s delay. Given the new
Rules in many provinces stressing speed, and fairly recent Supreme Court of Canada criminal
decisions, that lax approach to civil delay should stop now.⁷
Now, R.4.31 is inﬂuenced by Rr.1.2,
4.1, 4.2, by and the new culture shift for timely and affordable access to the system, and avoiding
unnecessary expense and delay.⁸
Delay is now anathema.⁹
Part 4, Division 6 generally, and R.4.31 speciﬁcally, emphasize prejudice. Traditionally, the
courts applied a three-part test to applications under R.4.31’s predecessors to dismiss for delay:¹⁰
1. is there inordinate delay,
2. which is inexcusable, and
3. which creates a likelihood of signiﬁcant prejudice?¹¹

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.31(3)
However, since 2010, R.4.31 looks at excuse only if the defendant wants to invoke a
presumption of prejudice, not in a case where the defendant gives evidence of prejudice.¹
On
excuses, see further Parts D.2 and D.4 below.
Intent matters; a lawsuit is an active process, and must not be a mere negotiation tactic.²
A review of the history shows that the functional approach to R.4.31 is the correct one.³
There is a suggestion that using this Rule is “highly discretionary” and nuanced, not a bright-
line test.⁴
This Rule also applies to Family Law and Divorce Act suits.⁵
C. Defendant’s Role
On waiver, see R.4.32. On defendants’ requests, see subpart D.2 below.
A defendant is not to encourage delay in the hope of a later application to dismiss, which may
be barred where the defendant did something to indicate acquiescence in the suit’s proceeding.⁶
But mere participation in proceedings does not bar such an application, nor undo delay.⁷
Nor does
participation in case management.⁸
It is the plaintiff who must take the initiative in keeping the action moving along.⁹

has no responsibility to move an action along,¹⁰

his own delay, and will not be allowed to complain if he refused to cooperate, or frustrated the

That can be seen as making the delay not inordinate.²
However, the plaintiff’s

The defendant’s delays are irrelevant, if they were about some tangent and did not delay
preparation for trial.⁴
The defendant cannot be blamed for delay if the plaintiff did not tell the defendant that the
plaintiff had ﬁnished some step (here examination for discovery) and so the defendant should
start its part.⁵
Waiting for the defendant to answer undertakings cannot be relied on by the plaintiff,
if he has certiﬁed in a Form 37 that all undertakings have been discharged.⁶
The defendant’s rôle
also can have indirect relevance. If the defendant in the suit is trying to conceal the defendant’s
pre-suit misconduct, that makes the suit more complex, and may justify slower progress by the
plaintiff.⁷
Disagreement by the defendant is not enough; the defendant would have to have failed to
carry out its procedural duties or refused to take certain steps.⁸

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.31(3)
delaying action. As it is the duty of the plaintiff (not the defendant) to prosecute the action, that
cannot refer to both the plaintiff and the defendant doing nothing where nothing prevents action
by the plaintiff, e. g. where neither side does or says anything about questioning (examination for
discovery).
If defendants and third parties are slow, the plaintiff should not just wait a long time; after a
while the plaintiff must take steps to speed up the process.¹
Especially after a single failure by the
defendant to answer one query.²
See also Part D.2 below.
Even if both sides were to a degree unreasonable, if the defendant did not really cause or
lengthen the delay, the defendant can rely on the delay under this Rule.³
Exceeding the time limit under R.4.31 has no automatic effect, and that Rule requires an
application to dismiss for want of prosecution. In the meantime, the action continues, and an
Applications Judge has jurisdiction to make usual sort of orders.⁴
Delay in moving to dismiss, and cooperating with the plaintiff in the suit, were held to bar a
motion to dismiss.⁵
But does that not set up two Catch 22’s, and contradict a great deal of case
law?
The defendant’s agreeing to delay questioning (examination for discovery) meant that delay
was not inexcusable.⁶
Failure to complain is neither acquiescence nor implied waiver.⁷
D. Delay and Excuses
1. Basic Test for Undue Delay
(a) General Approach
Rule 4.31 refers to delay in an action, so the entire action needs review, not just segments.
What is undue delay is a question of degree, which cannot be precisely deﬁned, and assessing it
includes the experience of the judiciary.⁸
A very useful procedure to test the plaintiff's inordinate delay, is to compare the time actually
taken to get a certain stage, with how long a reasonably diligent litigant would take in a similar
litigation, and with timelines in the Rules; complex matters take longer.⁹

R.4.31(3) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
(b) Indicia
Rule 4.31 asks whether a reasonable litigant’s speed in conduct of the suit has been met, if
the shortfall is inordinate,¹
whether any inordinate delay has been explained so as to justify it,²
whether an important interest of the defendant moving has been impaired enough by the delay to
override the plaintiff’s right to judgment on the merits, whether the presumption of prejudice has
been rebutted, and whether there is a compelling reason for not dismissing. (The last question
comes from the word “may” in R.4.31(1). )³
How close the case is to being ready for trial, is relevant.⁴
And whether the suit was becoming
closer to readiness for trial or simpler and easier to try, is also very relevant.⁵
Other possibly relevant factors⁶
for assessing undue delay may be:
1. complexity of the matter and similar aspects of the particular case,⁷
2. number of parties,
3. health of witnesses,
4. proceedings taken along the way,⁸
5. whether there have been long periods of inactivity,⁹
6. whether defendant obstructed plaintiff’s progress,
7. whether all the evidence is in documents,¹⁰
and

examination) is Condo. Plan 7920829 v. Academy Constr. (AJ) 2023 ABKB 244, JCE 1003 18358 (Apr
25) (¶’s 52-59). But this comparison with the diligent litigant is not the only valid measure: Thoreson v. Alta.
Infrastructure 2020 ABCA 146, Calg 1901 0052 AC (Apr 15) (¶’s 27-29). That the suit should have moved
faster is not enough; R.4.31 requires inordinate delay. But not using simpler, faster procedures is relevant:
Ouellette v. Repchuk (M) 2019 ABQB 329, JCC 1401 03523 (May 6).

8. whether the plaintiff alleges fraud.¹
Cases using this traditional test only relate to delay after commencement of the suit, not delay
before commencement.²
But under this Rule, the court looks at progress and delay during the
whole suit.³
That delay is “signiﬁcant” can refer to its length or to its bad effects on the suit or on the
litigants and their affairs; so what is a good enough excuse depends on the length of the delay.⁴
The court will not accept or rely on evidence that this degree of delay is common in Alberta; that
would reverse the proper philosophy.⁵
Expert evidence of what delay is undue is not necessary;
the record, counsel’s arguments, and the judiciary’s sufﬁce.⁶
(c) Which Rule?
If there is repeated delay or slow movement, but no 3-year gap between signiﬁcant advances,
then R.4.31 is the Rule for the defendant to rely on.⁷
Delay shorter than that which would trigger
R.4.33 can sufﬁce to dismiss under R.4.31.⁸
A history of the plaintiff’s dragging out the suit and
often coming close to missing deadlines or having the suit dismissed, is very relevant.⁹
No gap
without any action is needed to trigger R.4.31, but such a gap for some years is very relevant.¹⁰
(d) Total Time
Rule 4.31 involves no ﬁxed time for what is inordinate delay,¹¹
but total time elapsed, and
number and length of intervening delays are highly relevant. Lapse of 18-20 years since the
events sued over, is very important.¹²
Several examples of important steps not even suggested
or requested by the plaintiff for 6-8 years after they became possible and obvious, constituted
inordinate delay.¹³
Eleven years in the suit and not ready for trial, with 14-17 years elapsed since
the event in question, is too long.¹⁴
So is the nature of the suit, e. g. whether one had to wait
some time to see the degree of loss by the plaintiff and whether the claim would more or less go

Whether or not the suit is complex, and many people must be questioned (examined for
discovery), is relevant.²
The analysis is nuanced and highly discretionary.³
Some Masters had suggested a rule-of-thumb or presumption making 10 years’ history (or

, and is now held to be incorrect.⁵
(e) Miscellaneous
Doing the bare minimum to avoid dismissal under R.4.33, may trigger dismissal under R.
4.31.⁶
If some delay by the plaintiff is said to contravene the Rules of Court or a Practice Direction,
that is relevant under R.4.31, but the relevant Rule is that in force at the time of the delay, not the
Rule in force now.⁷
2. Requests by the Defendant for Progress
The plaintiff’s failing to do something necessary for over two years despite defence requests
to take that step, was a big factor in dismissing the suit.⁸
Cf. Part C above.
If inadvertence by the defendant causes some improper delay in the plaintiff’s lawsuit, after a
time it becomes the plaintiff’s duty to follow up, and take steps to end that delay.⁹
3. Connected Suits
Letting a parallel suit run and negotiations over using its questioning (discoveries) in some
cases, could excuse some years’ inaction in this suit.¹⁰
But generally a parallel suit is no excuse for
delay, if it does not prevent moving this suit along.¹¹

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.31(3)
Semble the belief by one side that the suit would pause while a side dispute was being
resolved, was no excuse.¹
4. Excuses
The Court of Appeal criticizes attempts to blame the plaintiff’s lawyer, or to divorce the plaintiff
from his lawyer’s neglect; if someone must suffer it is better the plaintiff who had a right and duty
to control and supervise his lawyer, rather than having the powerless defendant suffer; any other
rule is a “charter for the incompetent”.²
Retaining new counsel or delay by previous counsel is not
an excuse for inaction; new counsel take the suit as it is.³
Being self-represented is not an excuse
for undue delay by a plaintiff, especially if she had counsel or legal advice for much of the time.⁴
The reason for the delay is expressly relevant and important.⁵
The reason for the delay would
also inﬂuence the court’s decision how to formulate a remedial order.⁶
Enough excuse for a short
delay is not enough excuse for a long delay.⁷
Lack of an explanation for the delay is a weighty
factor against the plaintiff.⁸
A proper reason was needed, not a partial one.⁹
Weak excuses are
not enough, nor are excuses which have expired, i. e. after their proper effects have ceased for
some time.¹⁰
Lack of bad intent or lack of wilfulness, is of little signiﬁcance.¹¹
Inordinate delay puts the onus on the plaintiff to excuse it.¹²
The onus is on the plaintiff to prove
any excuse for his delay.¹³

4-80
R.4.31(3) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
Absence from Canada did not sufﬁce as an excuse.¹
Nor overwork.²
But being in jail does?³
Nor is an order to split trial an excuse, if neither issue is stayed or to await the other.⁴
Nor is a
Rule on mandatory mediation, since mediation and preparing for trial can be simultaneous.⁵
Poor
health could be an excuse for a while, but not for 14 years, especially without medical evidence.⁶
Nor vague claims of depression or illness which did not prevent other activities.⁷
The same is
true of illness of counsel.⁸
Numerous parties are little excuse before the stage of the suit for
questioning, absent any request to the court for case management or a litigation plan or to compel
some action.⁹
Pending legislation to remove a possible obstacle to the suit, can be an excuse.¹⁰
The suit should not be seen as a single track of sequential steps; a hitch in one does not
excuse stalling all others.¹¹
The steps in a suit are ordinarily independent, so the plaintiff need
not complete one before starting another.¹²
A string of mishaps are not an excuse, if the plaintiff
thereafter did little to cure them.¹³
Is delay for settlement discussions an excuse?¹⁴
Mental illness and inability of the litigant to get his ﬁles could excuse delay?¹⁵
If the plaintiff relies on time taken to get a report, one must see whether any other party had
the ability to get it, and how strong and close to the suit’s major issues are its conclusions.¹⁶
Non-
cooperation of an agent or ofﬁcer is not an excuse, if the plaintiff had legal means to enforce
co-operation.¹⁷
If a plaintiff did not sue for years after the events (e. g. because of late discovery of a problem),
that may make speed in the suit more important, not less, and make delay in the suit more
harmful, in many ways.¹⁸

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.31(3)
Institutional delay is not always counted against the plaintiff.¹
Indeed never?²
The topic of excuse covers not only the reason for the plaintiff’s delays, but also adequacy of
response to an unexpected delaying event.³
Trying to cure the problem sued over, by solicitors’ work instead of pressing on with the suit,
may be sensible for a while, but not for 16 years.⁴
Delay by the plaintiff at the defendant’s request is not presumed.⁵
It is also relevant that the
defendant did not cooperate in or fulﬁl its duties in, steps by the plaintiff which the defendant was
expressly seeking.⁶
Failure to complain of the delay is neither acquiescence nor implied waiver.⁷
Where it took the plaintiff years to realize that the Administrator of the Motor Vehicle Accident
Claims Act was really adverse and not his ally, that excused many years of inordinate delay.⁸
Where a summary-judgment justice found liability and told the parties to try to agree on
quantum, that made liability for any later non prosecution joint and barred use of R.4.31?⁹
Sometimes the chance just to bring a new action for the same thing is given as an excuse not
to dismiss the ﬁrst suit, but the Saskatchewan Court of Appeal pokes holes in that suggestion.¹⁰
5. Examples
One must be careful of how one uses examples from past cases, especially ones when it was
customary to ignore R.4.31 or its predecessor. One has to decide each case on its own merits,
not by citing factual precedents or numerical awards thought to be similar.¹¹
Cases merely making
factual decisions are not always cited.
There are many cases illustrating what is inordinate delay.¹²

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.31(3)
A history of repeated past delay is a bad sign.¹
So are past orders calling for more speed.²
The
plaintiff’s agreement to dates and then later saying still not ready, exacerbates the situation,³
as
does ignoring a previous consent order for sequence.⁴
Some years with no advance is signiﬁcant.⁵
Or the plaintiff deliberately stalling his own lawsuit.⁶
A suit brought by Originating Application should move more quickly.⁷
So should an appeal.⁸
One still sees occasional cases ﬁnding excuses not to give the defendant relief despite the
plaintiff’s long delay.⁹
The plaintiff sought and got a litigation plan, which is not an admission of inordinate delay,
especially if the concern was re R.4.33; the plan mitigates the delay.¹⁰
That there were initially many defendants and issues, slowly reduced by settlements, summary
judgments and discontinuances, making the suit less complex, mitigated the 10 years’ delay
against some remaining defendants.¹¹
E. Prejudice to the Defendant
1. General
The words “signiﬁcant” and “material” in the former and the present Rules mean the same
thing.¹²
The prejudice is to be injury or damage more than minor or trivial, and important enough to
attach serious consequences to the plaintiff.¹³
The absence of, or elimination of, prejudice to the defendant used to be much more important
than the plaintiff’s having a good reason for the delay.¹⁴
Whether that was still so, was unclear for
a while,¹⁵
but now prejudice is the most important factor under R.4.31.¹⁶

4-84
R.4.31(3) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
Under the previous Rules, if delay in the litigation had caused prejudice to one of the parties
that could be eliminated by terms, the court would not dismiss the action except in very unusual
circumstances. But if the serious prejudice could not be cured, the suit would be dismissed. See
further Part F below.
Prejudice occurring too early or too late is often not acted on, on the theory that it was not
caused or made harmful by the plaintiff’s delay.¹
Sometimes the court constructs an imaginary
timeline for a trial date without delay, and then holds that loss of witnesses or evidence before
or after that imaginary trial date is irrelevant. If that window is made narrow, no dead or missing
witness remains relevant. That must be wrong.²
Prejudice is not enough under R.4.31; it must
have been caused by or contributed to by the relevant delay.³
However, if the plaintiff sues a long
time after the event, then delay during that suit and its prejudice may be irreparable, because the
alternative means of getting evidence are already gone.⁴
The fading of memories need not be linked to a speciﬁc period of delay.⁵
Though death of a
witness or party before suit is no direct prejudice from delay in the suit, indirect prejudice can
come from delay in the suit, if the only witnesses remaining are relative strangers relying on
memory.⁶
It is signiﬁcant that the defendant now complaining did not learn of a possible complaint about
its conduct, for over 10 years.⁷
Delay in a suit is made worse if the plaintiff’s pleadings allege serious misconduct by the
defendant, and so faster prosecution of such a suit than usually demanded, is necessary. Such
pleadings are likely to prejudice the defendant with stress, reputation injury, and stale or lost
evidence.⁸

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.31(3)
2. Presumption
All people lose accurate memory after some years.¹
The previous Rules provided for a presumption of prejudice where the delay was “inordinate
and inexcusable. ”²
Even without a true legislated presumption, inordinate delay was prima facie
evidence of serious prejudice.³
Rule 4.31(2) again says (after a short time without such words⁴
),
and presumes, that inordinate and inexcusable delay means that the prejudice is “signiﬁcant”. So
since 2013 the Rules have again made that true presumption express,⁵
which means that it is a
mandatory fact ﬁnding unless there is adequate rebutting evidence.⁶
Therefore, it takes little or no

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.31(3)
Nothing less than inordinate delay raises a true mandatory presumption of serious prejudice.¹
And it has to be inexcusable also to raise the presumption.²
A presumption of prejudice was rebutted because the witnesses were lost early in the dispute.³
A business is entitled to some peace of mind and closure of an issue after a certain time, even
if no evidence has been lost.⁴
3. Types of Prejudice
(a) Importance
The courts are becoming more sensitive to actual prejudice.⁵
(b) Common Types of Prejudice
Delay in suits has many evils.⁶
Relevant prejudice can be of any kind,⁷
substantive,⁸
procedural,⁹
or evidentiary.¹⁰
Prejudice most commonly arises from witnesses dying or becoming missing,¹¹
or moving

4-89
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.31(3)
Trying to brush aside death of a witness as also hurting the plaintiff¹
is illogical; the relevant
question is prejudice to the defendant, not who will win the suit. Before the delay, the plaintiff had
key live witnesses and corroborating records. After the delay, only the records. Do the records
tell as much as the witnesses would have? Are they as convincing? Are they contemporary? Are
they even admissible in evidence? See subpart 5 below. A plaintiff should not be dragged by the
defendant’s delay from having a clear case to a borderline case.
Some justices and Applications Judges try to ﬁnd no prejudice from death or loss of lay
witnesses because there are still records or "it is a records case".²
That is superﬁcial reasoning.
In many suits records are not enough to make live witnesses superﬂuous or marginal.³
For one
thing, few records are evidence of truth of their contents. The presumption of prejudice is rebutted
only by evidence showing no prejudice, or maybe only very small or inconsequential prejudice.
Events taking only a few minutes 12 years ago rely on memories, not records, and memories fade
in such a case.⁴
Almost any record can be explained or negated by a contemporary witness. Under our system,
witnesses testify live at trial. We do not even rely on a transcript of what they swore to a decade
ago. The witnesses no longer recall details and cannot be properly cross examined. See subpart
3(d) below. And it is rare that remaining records cover fully all the major issues in the suit, so
presence of records usually has very limited use.⁵
The issue is not who will win at trial, nor is
the test clear failure. If the plaintiff goes from certain victory to possible victory, that is serious
prejudice.
Besides, how can the plaintiff prove the quality and content of what the dead would have
testiﬁed? That is a cross Catch-22. See subpart 4 below.
Similarly, excusing death of an important defence witness because the defendant has other
witnesses for that topic⁶
seems improper for the same reason. How can one say other witnesses
will render loss of this witness insigniﬁcant, without hearing any of them, when the defendant
cannot call evidence from the deceased even for this application, especially where the witness’
knowledge is about the key or only issue?
(c) Indirect Prejudice
If the slow lawsuit keeps a caveat on someone else’s title to land, especially if court-imposed
conditions are not being met, then even one year’s inaction may be one (among several) reasons
to dismiss the suit.⁷
In a suit to support a caveat, the fact the plaintiff did nothing for a year was

one of a number of reasons to dismiss the suit.¹
The Builders’ Lien Act s.33(2) has its own time

Allegations of professional misconduct hanging over the head of a law ﬁrm for 16 years, are

Impairment of the defendant’s business by such long-pending litigation is relevant
prejudice.⁴
Something hanging over one’s head is less serious where liability is not in issue, only

(d) Fading Memories
Obviously memories of complex matters fade and even distort badly after some years, so
the court should give that serious weight.⁶
Many people legally trained, oddly used to discount
heavily that presumption or danger.⁷
Now increasingly, stale memories are presumed and rightly
regarded as serious prejudice.⁸
Loss of some relevant evidence not recorded anywhere is enough
prejudice to dismiss.⁹
And on survival of some records, see subpart 3(b) supra.
It is very important to see how old are the events being litigated over and which would have to
be investigated at a trial.¹⁰

Hldg. v. All Steel Craft Fabr. (M) 2021 ABQB 268, JCE 1303 01587 (Apr 8) ; WestJet v. ELS Mktg.2023
ABKB 408, JCC 1001 00525 (Jul 5) (¶’s 69-70). Especially with a case based on oral agreements: M. L.
Bruce v. Ceco Dev. , supra, Part B. Less so in a straightforward debt action: Forest Res. Improvement
Assn. v. Moore, supra, Part B.
7 29 years since events in question and 19 years since commencement of suit, were excused because of
acquiescence, in 422252 Alta. v. Messenger 2019 ABQB 251, JCE 0003 22636. Quaere. Cf. Alston v.
Foothills (M. D. ) 2021 ABQB 592, JCC 1001 10069 (Jul 28). Quaere.
8 Tamaya Wynd Ests. v. Condo. Corp.072774, supra, subpart 3(b) ; 422252 Alta. v. Messenger (M) 2018

4-91
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.31(3)
Long delay is especially bad where an important factual issue is the state of mind of the
defendant or its then employees.¹
(e) Rebutting Prejudice?²
The presumption (or proof) of prejudice is not rebutted by saying that those witnesses gave
satisfactory evidence years ago (which means they now rely on an old transcript).³
On presence of records as a supposed cure for delay and prejudice, see subpart (b) above.
(f) Inherent Prejudice
Any suit which may succeed or fail creates prejudice to the defendant after a time; the prejudice
is acute if the delay impairs the defendant from meeting any burden of proof.⁴
Or if it is many years
before the defendant gets his ﬁrst chance to examine the plaintiff about the facts of years ago.⁵
The stigma of being accused of fraud arises from commencement of the suit, and so only
exacerbation, not the original prejudice, is relevant under this Rule. The same is true of prejudice
from defence costs incurred, especially if costs may largely remedy them if fraud was alleged
without a proper foundation.⁶
4. Evidence
See Part G below.
It is illogical to ask the defendant to give precise details of the evidence lost, still less how it
would have helped the defendant; that is a Catch-22, and usually the details of the evidence are
lost too.⁷
Occasionally, the defendant has direct evidence of prejudice, such as a recent cross-
examination of two of plaintiff’s witnesses who seem not to remember anything.⁸
5. Amelioration
An occasional case excuses prejudice to the defendant by presuming more prejudice to the
plaintiff from the delays,⁹
but that is illogical¹⁰
and seems contrary to most of the case law. Prejudice
is about the defendant’s evidence, not the plaintiff’s evidence.¹¹
It is particularly illogical to assume
that delay harms plaintiffs more than defendants, because often a prima facie case is easy to

R.4.31(3) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
provide, and proving the defence is the real issue.¹
And often a defendant then has a duty to
disprove a negative, which makes prejudice to the defendant more likely.²
In any event, prejudice
to the defendant does not disappear because there is also prejudice to the plaintiff. (That a plaintiff
stabbed a defendant would not be harmless because that plaintiff also cut himself. ) An application
to dismiss a suit is not a wager on a horserace; it is about prejudice to the defendant from the
plaintiff's poor conduct.
Whether dismissal now would have any practical beneﬁt is up to the defendant moving to
dismiss, and the court should not second-guess that.³
Nor was there any evidence that anyone
other than the plaintiff had any interest in re-suing.
On records supposedly supplementing witnesses, see also subpart 3(d), (e), above.
One case seems to hint that the defendant caused the prejudice by not identifying and
preserving evidence, relying in part on counsel’s submissions, not evidence of that.⁴
Sed quaere.
Even if the case is about ready for trial, that cannot undo the results of a litigation strategy
bringing the administration of justice into disrepute.⁵
F. Terms and Conditions
1. Need for Terms
Justices and Applications Judges refusing to dismiss the suit rarely do anything meaningful
on this topic, maybe because the counsel for the defendant neglects it or leaves the topic vague.
A failed attempt to draft terms to remove prejudice, or the harshness of such terms, shows how
necessary is dismissing the suit.
It is rarely proper for a justice or Applications Judge merely to refuse to dismiss the suit for
delay; usually at least some remedial procedural order is appropriate.⁶
The relevant question is
whether some lesser remedy than dismissing the suit will remedy the prejudice.⁷
It would appear
that defendants moving under this Rule forget that and very rarely seek such terms, even if the
Justice or Applications Judge proposes not to dismiss the suit.
Where one of the parties has suffered prejudice, the other party should suggest speciﬁc
terms and conditions that will negate or mitigate the prejudice that has been suffered.⁸
If no one
suggests terms to remedy prejudice, it is hard to complain about the terms which the chambers
judge picks.⁹

1 Proving a prima facie case of an old loan or debt is often easy: a cancelled cheque, or a promissory note.

of the collision and injury is easy, and a “yield” sign is easy; precisly who did what is not. In a professional
negligence suit, injury to the client or patient is often easy to prove. See also Condo. Plan No.7920829 v.
Academy Constr. , infra.
