PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.31(3)
2. Types of Terms
The court’s jurisdiction to overcome prejudice is very wide.¹
A schedule may be imposed
at once.²
If terms to undo prejudice were not imposed in King's Bench, the case may be sent
back by the Court of Appeal to do that. The same is true of terms to prevent further delay.³
It is
sometimes better to allow the action to proceed on stiff terms than either to dismiss the suit for
want of prosecution without terms, or simply to allow it to proceed without terms. Remedies are
mandated; in one case, solicitor-client costs of this motion and of the case management, and
capping prejudgment interest at 3 years.⁴
Stiffer terms were added by the Court of Appeal as a condition of letting a suit proceed despite
delay in its prosecution. Enough terms must be imposed to undo all prejudice.⁵
A timetable was to
be set. The defendant would get solicitor-client costs of any additional expert evidence necessary,
and would get adverse inferences from failure to call witnesses who were available before the
delays, and the defendant could give some hearsay.⁶
If the evidence negatives all prejudice, the
terms need not address it, but other terms must be imposed to guard against further delay.⁷
If a previous order extending time provided that the matter would automatically be struck if
the new time limits were not observed, then that sufﬁces to strike, and no ﬁnding of contempt is
necessary.⁸
The terms can include loss of pre-judgment interest, security for costs, and written notice of
intent to proceed with the suit.⁹
The order need not be conﬁned to the issue of prejudice. For example, the awarding of costs
allows the court to express its disapproval of the party who has caused the delay. A party guilty
of inordinate delay may have to pay costs even if his suit is allowed to continue.¹⁰
The court could
impose terms which will speed up the litigation, and prevent further delay.¹¹
Doubt as to whether the plaintiff would pay costs justiﬁes an order for security for costs, if the
suit is not dismissed.¹²

4-94
R.4.31(3) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
3. Cross-Claims
Former R.244.2 allowed terms or conditions on dismissal under the drop-dead Rule where
there were cross-actions, or a counterclaim, or a plea of set-off. There is no equivalent Rule in the
new Rules, leaving a mystery¹
which is not really resolved here. If the main claim and counterclaim
arise from the facts, since the plaintiff by counterclaim has a duty to move its counterclaim along,
the court can dismiss both the main claim and the counterclaim for delay.²
4. Miscellaneous
The court should not give an order for a litigation plan before the parties have tried to negotiate
one. The fact that there is a pending application to dismiss the action for want of prosecution does
not change that, as if it is dismissed for want of prosecution, the application will become moot.³
G. Evidence for Application
A former Rule permitted the use of afﬁdavits containing information and belief.⁴
This is now
gone.⁵
So is the old Rule which provided that the parties could be examined on oath to obtain
further evidence to be used on the motion.⁶
On the presumption of prejudice, see subpart E.2 above. On poor evidence of incapacitating
illness, see Part D above. On evidence of prejudice, see subpart E.4 above. On requiring evidence
of the contents of lost evidence, see subpart E.3(d) above.
Because of the importance of prejudice under this Rule, often the afﬁdavits and materials ﬁled
with the court on an application should emphasize that issue.⁷
The party moving to dismiss an
action for want of prosecution should specify the type of prejudice suffered, and should give some
particulars as to any steps taken to relieve that prejudice.⁸
For example, it is not sufﬁcient just to
depose that a witness seems to be missing; it is necessary to outline the steps that were taken in
attempting to locate the witness.⁹
Is it also necessary to prove that the prejudice was linked to the delay, and not caused by some
collateral factor?¹⁰
During one plaintiff’s long delay, medical evidence was lost, so the defendant
was prejudiced, and the suit was dismissed.¹¹
It is very common that a suit is dismissed because
a witness died or disappeared, or documents were destroyed or lost, during the inordinate delay.¹²

4-95
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.31(3)
After two interrelated suits were ordered tried together, there was delay and one was dismissed
for want of prosecution. That meant the elements of that were present in the other suit. Because of
their link, prejudice in defending one would be prejudice in defending the other.¹
The application is based on evidence about procedure. If the correspondence between the
lawyers seems relevant, it may be put into evidence by an afﬁdavit of a paralegal or employee of
one of the law ﬁrms. The client would probably lack enough knowledge to swear to that.²
H. Miscellaneous
After a divorce judgment has been granted, and a corollary relief variation order was granted,
it is too late to move to dismiss the divorce action for non prosecution. Rules 4.31 and 4.33 do
apply to divorce suits, but where the divorce judgment had been granted and corollary relief ruled
upon, there was no outstanding action left to strike or dismiss.³
But judgment for only some of the
relief requested, with the rest not yet granted or denied, does not make R.4.31 or 4.33 cease to
apply; the plaintiff still has a duty to prosecute the suit.⁴
For years Alberta courts were unable to deal with matters in a timely way because of chronic
under-support by both levels of government. Where children are involved, they deserve better
than that.⁵
The remedy for non-prosecution is dismissal under these Rules, not striking out under R.
3.68.⁶
Is delay by the defendant in moving under R.4.31, a reason to withhold relief and not dismiss
the suit?⁷
If delay by the defendant in moving were a bar, then the defendant would have to wait
just long enough but not too long. That is a recipe for Goldilocks’ porridge, not for law courts.
Though R.4.31 covers divorce suits, it only applies to current ones.
Is it generally improper to start a suit with no intent to carry it through to trial?⁸
A Cabinet Minister’s suit to forfeit property used for crime is subject to Rr.4.31 and 4.33,
because the legislation makes civil procedure and the Rules of Court apply, and R.1.2 calls for
timely resolution, and indeed such a suit especially needs expedition.⁹
If a party buys the plaintiff’s cause of action and takes over the existing suit by the plaintiff,
one cannot ignore delay in the action before that assignment. An assignee takes subject to all the
frailties of the assignor’s position.¹⁰
The assignee has to move to continue the suit, or the defendant
has to move to dismiss it under R.4.31.¹¹

R.4.32 PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
Rule 4.31 is not necessarily excluded by an order setting a trial date or adjourning a trial date,
and so an Applications Judge can hear a non-prosecution and dismissal application. The trial
judge need not hear such an application. Non-prosecution and dismissal are not part of the trial
process. Nor does a Form 37 setting down for trial and waiving further applications or steps, bar
a R.4.33 application. That ﬁling a Form 37 lets the plaintiff delay for years, would contravene the
foundational Rules saying that purpose and intent include timely resolution, and violate R.1.2(2)
(3).¹
There was no point in dismissing matrimonial actions for want of prosecution, as the limitation
periods had not run and the plaintiff could just commence a new action?²
But see Part I below.
Whether the necessary elements for dismissal under R.4.31 exist are largely questions of fact
owed appellate deference, absent palpable and overriding error.³
On appeal, it may be difﬁcult
to upset the chambers judge’s assessment of the proper weight for the competing factors here.⁴
On counterclaims, see Part F above.
Multiple unsuccessful attempts by the defendant to dismiss the suit increased the costs
awarded to the plaintiff.⁵
If there is any discretion not to dismiss the action, it must be exercised judicially, not for a
merely harsh result, ignoring irrelevant matters and considering all relevant ones.⁶
A request to stay civil contempt proceedings is not dismissal of all or part of the suit, so R.4.31
has no application, and is no basis to allow leave to appeal.⁷
I. Results of Dismissal Order
Someone whose action was dismissed for want of prosecution with a ﬁnding of prejudice to
the defendants, cannot then resue for the same thing. That is an abuse of process, and may be
contempt if the ﬁrst judgment gave directions to be obeyed (removal of barriers etc. ).⁸
Agreement About Delay
4.32 If 2 or more parties agree to delay an application or proceeding in an action,
each of the other parties must be served with notice of the agreement to delay
and of the nature and extent of the delay.⁹

Related Provisions
4.4(2) (proposal as to timing of action) ; 13.5 (variation of time).

On express agreements, see R.4.33 n. E, infra.
Rule 4.32 was new in 2010.
Rule 4.32 lets the parties agree on a period of delay, if they specify the details and both parties
get a copy of the agreement, which must be express.
What would otherwise be undue delay dictating dismissal of a suit for want of prosecution, may
not be that, if there was a standstill agreement which has never been ended despite the fact of a
number of years having passed.¹
But waiver of past delay is not necessarily a waiver of future delay. A consent order to take
the next step, or a medical examination by the defendant, shows consent that the suit proceed
despite past delay. But further delay is a fresh situation.²
Silence is not acquiescence, and acquiescence is not express. A party not acquiescing does
not have to make his or her non-acquiescence express.³
A time waiver cannot be inferred from
mere conduct.⁴
Nor from a request for inaction for a week or two (which has long since passed).⁵
On waiver, see R.4.32. Because prejudice is presumed from delay, a party alleging waiver
has the onus of proving it.⁶
Is ﬁling a statement of defence and counterclaim waiver of plaintiff’s
delay?⁷
Acquiescence was argued, but in fact the discussion was about past events, not about the
plan to go forward, and preserving certain existing questioning rights. It was not an agreement by
all parties to avoid litigation steps entirely.⁸
Dismissal for Long Delay
4.33(1) In this rule
(a) “applicant” means a party to an action who makes an application to
dismiss the action for delay as set out in this rule;
(b) “respondent” means a party who has ﬁled a commencement
document;
(c) “suspension period” means, in subrules (5) to (9), a period that ends
on
(i) a speciﬁc date, or
(ii) the happening of a speciﬁc event.⁹

R.4.33(2) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
(2) If 3 or more years have passed without a signiﬁcant advance in an action, the
Court, on application, must dismiss the action as against the applicant, unless
(a) the action has been stayed or adjourned by order, an order has been
made under subrule (9) or the delay is provided for in a litigation plan
under this Part, or
(b) an application has been ﬁled or proceedings have been taken since
the delay and the applicant has participated in them for a purpose
and to the extent that, in the opinion of the Court, warrants the action
continuing.
(3) If the Court refuses an application to dismiss an action for delay, the Court
may make whatever procedural order it considers appropriate.
(4) The period of time referred to in subrule (2) does not include the following,
whichever ends earlier:
(a) the period of time between the service of a statement of claim on an
applicant and the service of the applicant’s statement of defence;¹
(b) the period of one year after the date of service of a statement of
claim on an applicant.
(5) If a respondent and an applicant agree in writing to a suspension period, the
period of time under subrule (2) does not include the suspension period agreed
to.
(6) A respondent, whether or not the respondent has sought agreement under
subrule (5), may serve an applicant with a written proposal setting out a
suspension period and requesting that the suspension period not be included
in computing the period of time under subrule (2).
(7) An applicant who receives a proposal referred to in subrule (6) must respond
within 2 months, and within that time
(a) if the applicant agrees with the proposal, then the period of time
under subrule (2) does not include the suspension period agreed to,
or
(b) if the applicant disagrees with the proposal or does not respond
to the proposal, then the respondent may apply for an order under
subrule (9).
(8) If a suspension period is agreed to under subrule (5) or (7) (a), the respondent
must give all other parties to the action written notice setting forth what the
suspension period is, when it was agreed to and by whom.
[Footnote 9 from prior page]

Related Provisions
6.3 (applications generally) ; 15.4 (transitional rule)

A. General Principles
1. History
This Rule is still mandatory,²
so it is often called “the drop-dead Rule”. It leaves no room for
discretion, and R.1.2 does not charge that.³
The former “drop dead” Rule had imposed a longer ﬁve-year mandatory limit on inaction. The
only signiﬁcant change of the 2010 Rule from the predecessor R.244.1⁴
was temporarily dropping
the time to 2 years and then ﬁnally moving it up to 3 years.⁵
A small change in the 2010 Rule’s
wording was immaterial.⁶
But then the Rule was rewritten in 2016.⁷
The Covid-19 virus epidemic and quarantine in the Spring of 2020 led to a Ministerial Order
27/2020 extending the time within which a step in a proceeding must be taken. But the court has
discretion in the matter. Might that apply to the 3-year period under R.4.33?

Rule 4.31 still exists, and Rules 4.31 and 4.33 have different functions.¹
Prejudice is vital to R.
4.31, but irrelevant to R.4.33.²
There is a general article published.³
2. Advancing Resolution of the Action⁴
(a) Conditions
Rule 4.33(3), about need to impose conditions to letting the suit proceed, is often improperly
overlooked.⁵
It is rarely proper merely to dismiss an application under R.4.33; usually a remedial
procedural order then is called for.⁶
If the suit is not dismissed, conditions such as a schedule,
may be imposed at once.⁷
(b) Rule is Mandatory and Important
If nothing has been done to signiﬁcantly⁸
advance resolution of the action for three years,⁹
then the court must¹⁰
dismiss the action.¹¹
Rule 4.33 is mandatory,¹²
and allows no discretion. Merits

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.33(10)
claim, are irrelevant. Nor are actions not actually performed.¹
It does not require any inquiry into
prejudice.²
Outcomes should not be overemphasized.³
The new general and increased willingness of courts to give summary remedies and not to
lean heavily toward trial anymore, is relevant also to dismissal for non-prosecution.⁴
Trial is not
necessarily appropriate, nor the normal procedure.⁵
Delay in a suit is anathema to all the principles of the 2010 Rules (efﬁciency, proportionality,
and economy).⁶
The foundational Rules cannot override the clear language of R.4.33, though they
could be relevant to what steps advanced the lawsuit.⁷
Delay in litigation is serious across Canada,
and especially bad in Alberta with institutional delays; and the Supreme Court of Canada’s call for
a culture shift against delay is relevant.⁸
Access to courts is conditional on not abusing that right.⁹
(c) Need for an Application
However, the court need not dismiss the suit if no defendant moves to dismiss it, or to dismiss
the relevant part.¹⁰
The party moving to dismiss the suit has to lead evidence of no signiﬁcant advance in the right
period (though doing so may then put some evidentiary burden on the opposing party).¹¹
(d) Functional Test¹²
This new functional approach is important.¹³
Under it, the court must assess the whole picture
of events during the 3-year period, qualitatively.¹⁴
Everything going on during the three-year period

4-102
R.4.33(10) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
must be looked at. That the activity responds to a request by the other side is relevant, but no
longer conclusive, especially if there is a long delay after the request.¹
A combination of events
(no one sufﬁcient) can sufﬁce together to advance the suit enough.²
The test is one of substance, not form,³
and practical and qualitative, not mechanical.⁴
To see
whether resolution of the suit was actually signiﬁcantly advanced, is a functional examination
taking a macro contextual view of the suit; formulaic or legally-required steps or examination are
not enough.⁵
The steps to implement the functional approach and weigh what has been done, are
described.⁶
The functional approach to civil procedure is current presently. It allows one to gauge
progress by how well an event performs its function.⁷
The court looks at quality of steps taken through a trained lens, noting the nature, genuineness,
and timing of the steps in question.⁸
Under R.4.33, an ”advance” means a forward movement to effect a purpose. And “signiﬁcant
advance” means one which is important,⁹
weighty or notable.¹⁰
What was the nature, value, quality,
genuineness, timing, and (sometimes) outcome, of what was done?¹¹
The question is whether in
the relevant time something or things were done which increased by a measurable degree the
chances that the court can assess the merits, considering the trajectory and character of the suit.¹²

4-103
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.33(10)
The question is what actually resulted from some events; what good results could have
occurred is not a relevant question.¹
Generally, an incomplete step (such as an application not
heard or producing no order, or a useless order, a request not complied with, or an unsuccessful
attempt to schedule something or a proposed agreement), is not a signiﬁcant advance.²
Thinking
about a step, planning it, or discussing it, is not enough, just talk.³
Even a mere agreement to do it
is not enough.⁴
Nor is gathering information for the other side but not sending it to the other side,
especially if it is not clear whether the information collected would have been enough.⁵
An act may signiﬁcantly advance the suit, by reducing the number of defendants, and so
narrowing the issues and shortening the trial.⁶
But care is needed; one must use a functional
analysis, and dropping ﬂuff or trivialities or throw-away points does not advance the suit.⁷
Mere categories of activity, or looking for mandated steps which really are trivial, is no longer
the law.⁸
A legally-required step used to be considered to advance the action and restart the
clock.⁹
But that is no longer so, given a change in wording of the Rule. Now such a step’s actual
effects must be weighed.¹⁰
Finding a precedent about the particular step relied on in the case

4-104
R.4.33(10) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
at hand, is obsolete, given the functional approach and its contextually-sensitive nature.¹
For
example, an issue may be central, but a document provided about it may be peripheral.²
Now the
law is clariﬁed:³
whether something done by the plaintiff signiﬁcantly advanced resolution of the
suit is answered by a functional analysis, more than a menu of activities.⁴
So in deciding what is
a signiﬁcant enough advance, looking at past factual precedents is of some help, but they are
not determinative; each lawsuit is unique. The content, value, and timing of the advance in the
action must be assessed in the context of that particular suit. That is the heart of the functional
approach.⁵
Therefore subpart (e) “examples” below may have very limited use now.
Work by one side after does not advance the suit if the other side does not know about that
work.⁶
The thing advancing can be done by either party.⁷
The thing need not advance the action
against all defendants?⁸
And an advance against one part can advance the whole suit too, in
some cases.⁹
See also Part C.
An odd suggestion is that the action must have truly died to dismiss it,¹⁰
does not correspond
to the wording or binding case law in or under R.4.33.¹¹

receipt of enough important information); cf. Barnes v. RBC Dom. Secur. (Alta M 16 Nov ’05) JDE 9703

Ravvin Hldg. v. Ghitter 2008 ABCA 208, 437 AR 66; Leder v. Karoles (M) 2009 ABQB 334, [2010] 2 WWR
134; Le v.1055168 Alta. , supra. An advance order on how to handle undertakings advances the suit, even
if no undertakings are ever given? Weins v. Dewald, supra. Quaere. If parties are negotiating, getting
more medical information may advance not only negotiations, but also any later trial: Black v. Gossner
2010 ABQB 360, 494 AR 375. Otherwise there is no negotiating and the defendant knows nothing about
it: Goshulak v. Nguyen (M) 2011 ABQB 346, 520 AR 1. But incomplete and non-answers to undertakings
do not advance the suit: Davenport Homes v. Cassin, supra; cf. JDC Contr. v. Moose Mtn. Equip.2015
ABQB 98, 56 CPC(7th) 421 (dicta). But the answer may lie in the rest of this paragraph.
4 See subpart A.2 supra, especially the Ro-Dar and Weaver decisions in the Court of Appeal, supra.
5 Alderson v. Wawanesa Life Ins. Co. (M) 2019 ABQB 96, JCC 1201 02744 (Feb 13) (¶ 21).
6 Re Riehs Est. , supra (¶'s 25-37) ; Abou Shaaban v. Baljak 2024 ABKB 28, JCC 1701 15317 (¶’s 70-80,
82-86); Loncikova v. Goldstein 2023 ABCA 358, Calg 2201 0205 AC (Dec 13) (¶’s 10-15).

(e) Examples
It is questionable whether examples before 2016 are any longer useful. (They have now been
removed from this book but may be found in earlier editions. ) Now the analysis must be case by
case and functional, not by category, so even later ones may be merely factual decisions and not
good precedents, but some may give clues about what factors to consider or weigh.
There are many activities which after 2016 were held not to advance resolution of that action
nor stop the clock.¹

4-107
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.33(10)
to bar a motion to dismiss.¹
Merely agreeing to a case conference, without holding it, may achieve
nothing, or just delay the suit.²
Simply ﬁling and serving an interlocutory application but not taking it to any hearing, is not
sufﬁcient to advance the suit; still less is an “application” by letter to a judge with no copies to
other parties.³
Serving some types of notice is analogous to serving a notice to admit; the issue is whether
it had some signiﬁcant effect, e. g. an admission. But silence where no Rule deems that an
admission, has no effect, and notice does not stop time under R.4.33.⁴
(f) Advance What?
The issue is not merely doing something; it is moving the action along.⁵
The test is one of
substance, not form.⁶
An action can be signiﬁcantly advanced by activity, even if no settlement,
nor setting down for trial, is achieved.⁷
But attempts (however genuine) to settle do not materially
advance the suit unless they narrow the issues or produce relevant information.⁸
So outcomes
are relevant.⁹
The court emphasizes that the things done must in substance signiﬁcantly advance
the suit, looking at the nature, quality, genuineness, and timing of the things done. One looks
at the beginning and end of the period said to have had no signiﬁcant advance, to see what
difference there is beteween the two in ﬁnding the relevant facts and law which will affect the
ultimate resolution of the dispute.¹⁰
Very often a step which is proposed but neither agreed to, nor
acted upon, or which is never completed, does not signiﬁcantly advance the action.¹¹
The quality of
the work done in the 3-year period must be looked at.¹²
Action must be weighed in the context of its
own suit.¹³
There is no reason to let the action wither on the vine for years while the parties conduct
desultory negotiations; some degree of both negotiation and litigation is possible and common.

(g) Mandatory Steps
Warning: the cases formerly cited in this subpart 2(g) very likely do not survive the Court of
Appeal’s functional test described in subpart 2(d) above.⁵
(h) Linked Suits
If two proceedings are inseparably (“inextricably”) linked, advancing one may advance the
other and so stop time running.⁶
But there is no signiﬁcant advance in the second suit otherwise.⁷
The case law on how to analyze and weigh the linkage between the two suits is reviewed.⁸

issue in one suit may have to determine an issue in the other suit,¹
or bar deciding something;
in any event whether those are true is weighty, even if not individually decisive.²
The mere fact
that two events 3 years apart are broadly similar, does not mean that separate suits over the two
events are inextricably linked. There would have to be evidence that any result in one suit would
be legally or factually determinative, or relevant and binding, in the other suit. That there is some
cross-over evidence is not enough.³
On two closely-linked defendants in the same suit facing the
same (debt) claim, see the Palisade case.⁴
Gathering records for litigation, where two different suits are somewhat similar, for R.4.33
requires deciding which suit they were desired for.⁵
On a clump of related suits case managed for many years, see the Piikani case.⁶
(i) Miscellaneous
The exception in R.4.33(2) for a stay, refers to a stay of the whole action, not just a postponement
of the trial.⁷
Whether some activity during a suit signiﬁcantly advanced the suit or not, is a question of
mixed law and fact, and a decision about that by a chambers judge is owed deference on appeal.⁸
Though ambiguity in the wording of the Rule might be interpreted to favor the plaintiff, ambiguity
or doubt in the evidence or the facts brings no such presumption.⁹
An odd case featured purported emails and court documents which could not be found on the
court ﬁle or anyone else’s computer.¹⁰
In R.4.33, “respondent” is deﬁned as the party starting the lawsuit, and so such a person
is free at any time to move for a suspension period under subrule (9). Therefore, such a party
who did not earlier ask for such a suspension, should not and cannot be given a retroactive
suspension period.¹¹
A bulky lawsuit can be dismissed under R.4.33 in morning King’s Bench chambers; it needs no
special hearing, if the facts and issues for R.4.33 are not complex or bulky. That is especially true
if the judge reviews the afﬁdavits before the hearing. The decision not to adjourn is discretionary.¹²

4-110
R.4.33(10) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
3. No Excuses
It is doubtful that excuses for the delay are relevant under R.4.33. In any event, the court
and the defendant have no concern with the plaintiff’s shortage of resources or its priorities, or
how busy the plaintiff is.¹
Even if disaability is an excuse (which is doubtful), it would be irrelevant
unless it prevented the plaintiff from being able to retain and instruct counsel.²
Because this R.4.33 is mandatory,³
not discretionary,⁴
it does not have general exceptions.⁵
It
is neither moderated, nor given exceptions, by Rr.1.1, 1.2 nor 1.3, its wording being clear.⁶
There
is no discretion not to dismiss.⁷
It is not softened for those litigants without a lawyer.⁸
Excuses for the plaintiff’s delay, such as bad health or problems with his or her counsel, cannot
be raised after the time limit in R.4.33 has expired; a time extension or the like sought while time
is running, is the only relief.⁹
Merits of the claim, fairness, sympathy, prejudice, or justiﬁcation for
the delay are not relevant, nor is intent to prosecute the claim or intended actions not actually
performed.¹⁰
Justiﬁcation for the plaintiff’s delay is irrelevant.¹¹
It is irrelevant that the plaintiff’s
lawsuit is strong.¹²
Therefore some older cases ﬁnding excuses for the delay¹³
are probably now
wrong. Case management of the suit is no excuse.¹⁴
At all times, the plaintiff still has the initiative, even if both sides at times are slow in replying to
each other, so long as the defendant does not obstruct, stall or delay an action which the plaintiff

4-111
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.33(10)
is advancing.¹
The Rules do not require the defendant to help run or advance the suit.²
Some suits
have died and need to be pruned away.³
That some delay was not the plaintiff’s fault is irrelevant;
he or she still must advance the dispute.⁴
That the plaintiff was an infant and the limitation period has expired, may be an answer to a R.
4.31 motion, but not to a R.4.33 motion, whose result is compulsory.⁵
Is disability an exception to
R.4.33?⁶
Can illness suspend time if it produces mental incapacity?⁷
The whole approach in the Armitage decision seems contrary to most of this Part A.3.⁸
Much
the same is true of the topic of no limitation period, in the AIG decision.⁹
The Saskatchewan Court of Appeal has poked holes in the suggestion that a suit should not
be dismissed for non-prosecution because someone could sue afresh for the same thing.¹⁰
4. Miscellaneous
This R.4.33 applies like a statutory limitation period.
The onus is on the defendant moving to lead evidence to show that nothing (material) has
occurred for over 3 years.¹¹
The pre-2010 Rules had a Rule on when hearsay evidence could be
used under R.4.33’s predecessor, but oddly that Rule too was dropped in 2010, creating a very
predictable mystery or unfairness.¹²
A bar to further action in the suit is no excuse if the bar can only be removed by the plaintiff or
his lawyers, and nothing has been done about the bar.¹³

4-112
R.4.33(10) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
Under this Rule the strength or weakness of the case is irrelevant. This is the functional
approach.¹
Rule 4.33 does not apply to a completed lawsuit, with ﬁnal judgment entered and no issues
reserved.²
B. Calculating Time³
This Rule is about a gap between signiﬁcant advances in the suit.⁴
So when an important suit-
advancing event occurs, is important.⁵
Rule 4.33 is triggered only if 3 years elapse with nothing signiﬁcant done, before the defendant’s
application (notice of motion) to dismiss is ﬁled. It is not enough that 3 years elapse before the
court decides the application,⁶
even if the supporting evidence is ﬁled later than the application.⁷
So a new step by the plaintiff (such as disclosure), made after that ﬁling, has no effect.⁸
But can
the defendant just cure his problem with a new application?⁹
To calculate time, one counts forward from the date of the last noncontroversial signiﬁcant
advance. One does not count backward from ﬁling the motion, as the Rule’s wording shows.¹⁰
Because the relevant date is ﬁling the application, that the courts are not regularly sitting because
of an epidemic, is not relevant.¹¹
Rule 4.33(2) speaks of “3 or more years”, and R.13.4(3) on calculating by ﬁnding the day with
the same number applies to calculating years. Rule 4.33(2) sets the deadline at 3 years, not more
than 3 years. But the 3 years must have passed and do not expire until the end of the day with
the same number as the last helpful step. The plaintiff has all the anniversary day (with the same
number) to take another meaningful step.¹²

4-113
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.33(10)
The 3 years cannot be unilaterally extended by new activity without the other side’s consent
or acquiescence. The “drop-dead” Rule was originally designed to add teeth and certainty. Expiry
of 3 years should not merely launch a foot race to see which party can ﬁle an application ﬁrst.¹
The plaintiff’s application was a few days before the 3 years expired. When the Master heard
and adjourned the applications, the period of that delay should not be included, nor should the
time after the defendant ﬁrst ﬁled her application to dismiss.²
Any 3 years without a signiﬁcant advance triggers R.4.33, unless the defendant thereafter
participates in more steps to an extent making dismissal unjust.³
The entire gap or delay relied on by the defendant cannot have elapsed before the new Rule’s
shorter time period came into effect (to avoid retroactivity). Some of it must have elapsed under
(during) the old Rule.⁴
In computing this Rule’s 3 years, R.4.33(4) says not to count a period not exceeding the
year between statement of claim’s service and service of statement of defence. It lets the court
designate a date on which it would have been reasonable to ﬁle a defence, if none has been ﬁled.
If a defence has been ﬁled, then the court need not determine what is reasonable, and the 3 years
starts running from ﬁling the defence.⁵
Since R.4.33 does not include the time after service of
the statement of claim (up to one year), a judge thought that meant that time between issue and
service of the statement of claim did not count either.⁶
Subrule (4) only applies to proceedings
commenced by a statement of claim, not to Originating Notices.⁷
Because of Covid, a Ministerial Order suspended limitation periods in certain enactments,
and suspended times within which any step must be taken in any proceeding or suspended
proceeding. The enactments listed were numerous, and the suspension includes all regulations
under them, and one enactment listed was the Judicature Act. Rule 4.33 functions like a limitations
period. And even if that is not so, that Rule is covered by the reference to periods of time within
which any step must be taken.⁸
The 3-year period (as extended because of Covid) ran until the application for dismissal under
R.4.33 was ﬁled. From May 13 2020, one could not directly apply in a family matter until one got
permission from Family Docket Court. That was given after the Notice to Attend Family Docket
Court said that a motion under R.4.33 was desired, though it did not give details. That was the
“ﬁling” date applicable to stop time running. Any delay after that was the court’s, not any party’s.⁹

4-114
R.4.33(10) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
Rule 4.33 is not necessarily excluded by an order setting a trial date or adjourning a trial date.
An Applications Judge can hear a non-prosecution and dismissal application. The trial judge need
not hear such an application. Non-prosecution and its dismissal are not part of the trial process.
Rule 4.33 has an exception (subrule (2) (a) ) for an order which stays or adjourns the action, but
that is not the same as an order staying or adjourning the trial. Nor does a Form 37 setting down
for trial. That ﬁling a Form 37 lets the plaintiff delay for years, would contravene the foundational
Rules saying that purpose and intent include timely resolution, and violate R.1.2(2) (e).¹
Rule 4.33 requires some action within three years, but does not say where it is to occur, and
often there is more than one place that a given useful activity could be done. But if the nature of
the act (e. g. service) requires that it be done at a certain place, then s.22(2) of the Interpretation
Act can apply if the relevant location was closed on the date in question. Email service was not
agreed to here, and the law ofﬁce concerned was closed on the last day. But a fax number was
given, and faxes can be sent on a weekend or holiday, and “ofﬁce hours” do not apply to faxes.
Section 22(2) does not require that activity be possible only by one method or place, in order for
the subsection to apply and extend time.²
Filing an application to dismiss for long delay crystallizes the parties’ rights as of the date of
its ﬁling, and usually there should be no further steps in the action until the delay application is
decided.³
Time stops running once the court rules on or denies all aspects of remedies sought, but not
when only some remedies have been ruled on.⁴
C. Who is Affected
Does advancing the suit against one defendant stop time running against all defendants?⁵
Activity interrupting the three years and saving the suit can include steps by parties other than the
plaintiff,⁶
even if it relates only to some of the defendants.⁷
Rule 4.33 can be invoked by a defendant who has been noted in default, and also by the
Administrator of the Motor Vehicle Accident Claims Act, who stands in the shoes of such
defendant. Noting in default admits liability, but does not waive diligence, nor bar applications by
the defendant.⁸
The Rule applies to anyone with a duty to prosecute the suit, even a defendant whom the court
ordered to take conduct of the suit, so after 2 years his defence may be struck.⁹
But a plaintiff
cannot use this Rule to have her own suit dismissed, even to avoid costs usually payable on
discontinuance.¹⁰
There is a discussion of the principles of who must be involved in the thing done,
in the Tire City case.¹¹

4-115
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.33(10)
Exceeding the time limit under R.4.33 has no automatic effect, and it takes an application
to dismiss for want of prosecution.¹
In the meantime, the action continues, and an Applications
Judge has jurisdiction to make usual sort of orders.²
An application under R.4.33 is only for the
beneﬁt of the party applying?³
If only one defendant moves to dismiss, the written application
can be amended to add the other defendants as applying, but evidence is necessary as to their
involvement, e. g. common owner.⁴
Crown corporations are not bound by limitation periods.⁵
If the plaintiff is an agent of the
Crown, then R.4.33 is like a limitation period and does not bind the plaintiff; besides, it would be
pointless, as the plaintiff could resue anyway.⁶
D. Special Types of Suit
A Cabinet Minister’s proceeding (under forfeiture of property used for crime legislation) is
not immune from R.4.31 or R.4.33. Ordinarily it takes special legislation to bind the Crown, but
here this legislation’s s.51 expressly states that ordinary rules and procedure in civil cases, and
“the Alberta Rules of Court” apply to such actions. And R.1.2 calls for timely and cost-effective
resolution. Expedition is important where the government wishes to conﬁscate private property.⁷
An aboriginal rights suit against the Crown is not immune from this Rule.⁸
Nor is a divorce or
matrimonial property suit.⁹
Two years’ inaction killed a divorce and matrimonial property suit.¹⁰
There is no exception for a suit stayed by court order, where the plaintiff could have ended he
stay by some fairly easy step.¹¹
The exception in R.4.33(2) (a) is only for stay of the whole action, not for adjournment of
an application.¹²
A foreclosure suit often has both in rem and in personam (personal liability)
claims. An order nisi against one defendant only, with no personal liability of that defendant, does
not decide the in personam claims, nor stop time running for them.¹³
This Rule does apply to a
separate cause of action contained in a statement of claim which also forecloses on a mortgage.¹⁴

4-116
R.4.33(10) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
Although the word “action” is widely deﬁned, neither a probate caveat nor an application for
advice and directions, nor a trial of an issue under it, are covered by R.4.33, if no issues have
been directed to be tried yet.¹
After a divorce judgment was granted, and a corollary relief variation order was granted, it is
too late to move to dismiss the divorce action for non-prosecution. Rules 4.31 and 4.33 do apply
to divorce suits,²
but where the divorce judgment had been granted and corollary relief ruled upon,
there was no outstanding action left to strike or dismiss.³
Rule 9.21 on renewing judgments has its
own 10-year time limit, so R.4.33 does not apply to it.⁴
In a ﬁght inside a company, valuations can be central, and it may be signiﬁcant that the
applicant has not yet hired a valuator.⁵
If a party buys the plaintiff’s cause of action and takes over his existing suit, the court cannot
ignore delay in the action before that assignment. An assignee takes subject to all the frailties of
the assignor’s position.⁶
Rule 4.34 on transfer or transmission of interest does not bar an application under R.4.33, nor
does the stay period created by R.4.34 count under a R.4.33 application.⁷
A Charter claim does not bypass R.4.33 if Canadian courts have already ruled on and rejected
such a claim.⁸
E. Agreements
1. References and History
On waiver and standstill agreements, see also the notes to R.4.4, supra. On the history, see
the footnote, supra, at the end of the text of R.4.33.
The Rule used to refer to an “express” agreement,⁹
but the 2016 amendment to the Rule
drops that word. However, it now adds a requirement that the agreement must be written. Case
law holding that a course of conduct does not sufﬁce, obviously still holds. And a telephone
conversation would not be effective. So probably some of the pre-2016 case law set out below
would still be valid, but some would not. And still the “agreement” must be express, even if some
details may be left to implication.¹⁰

1 Malhomme v. Merlin 2006 ABCA 361, 401 AR 71.

4-117
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.33(10)
2. Minimum Contents
Any agreement’s basic terms must be set out.¹
An exchange of correspondence will sufﬁce if
it is clear and precise enough.²
Parties, start of period, and essential terms must be spelled out.³
Recent changes to R.4.33 reﬂect the case law about the tests for a standstill agreement:
clear intent is needed, not inference or implication, and the basic agreement must be settled and
clear. Here a party gave appointments for discovery but said that he would adjourn the discovery
if all counsel agreed that the additional time would not be relied on under R.4.33, and then later
referred to R.4.33. Lengthy correspondence among the parties ensued. The agreement would
not reasonably go on forever, and the judge found it gave a standstill for only about 8 months.⁴
A mere request not to be noted in default is not a standstill agreement,⁵
it does not put the
onus on the defendant to advance the suit, and does not apply to the whole suit,⁶
and 3 years’
inaction after that is too long.⁷
Nor is some courtesy a standstill agreement, and the courts must
not discourage such courtesies.⁸
But if the defendant or someone acting in his place⁹
asks the
plaintiff to take no further steps without written notice, that creates a standstill waiver agreement
and a vague later letter does not end it? Rule 4.4 is excluded.¹⁰
The fact that for a short while the plaintiff was waiting to hear from the defendant, is not
enough to bar the defendant’s use of R.4.33; this is not like a request by the defendant to take
no further steps.¹¹

4-118
R.4.33(10) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
Failure to complain about delay is neither acquiescence nor implied waiver.¹
Silence is not
acquiescence in more delay,²
it not being an express standstill agreement.³
Is that still good law
after 2016?
A “written proposal. . . that the action not be advanced” under R.4.33(6) does not include an
Appointment for Questioning. And a written proposal has no effect until two months pass, so it was
to be served two months before the 3-year drop-dead period passes.⁴
3. What Does Not Sufﬁce
A temporary delay in a certain step, by agreement, cannot be a standstill agreement because
no other agreement beyond what is express, can be inferred.⁵
Merely adjourning indeﬁnitely an
obstreperous examination for discovery, is not an agreement to suspend the times under R.4.33.⁶
If there is a long delay by a consent adjournment then both parties are responsible for that delay
and so the plaintiff cannot escape being hurt by it.⁷
An agreement to suspend R.4.33, expressly must be in writing. Mere references in submissions
about the need to complete passing of accounts before a certain claim can be determined, do not
sufﬁce, even if there is now a written transcript of those submissions.⁸
Even entering into a litigation plan did not sufﬁce because one needs a useful step, not just a
plan or deadline to make such a step?⁹
Settlement discussions are not themselves an excuse for not advancing the suit, even if they
fail.¹⁰
Once the defendants told the plaintiffs they viewed the 3 years as having expired, they did not
have to reply to suggested further steps. The defendants gave notice they were going to move
under R.4.33 and were not waiving that or consenting to or opposing anything else.¹¹
4. Express vs. Implied
Whether there was an express agreement under R.4.33 was a question of fact, on which
the Court of Appeal owes deference to the chambers judge.¹²
Being express is no longer a legal
requirement, but whether there was a “written agreement to a suspension period” is presumably
also a question of fact owed similar deference.
5. Calculating Time
Parties can word a standstill agreement under R.4.33 for any result that they want, but if it is
not clear (or speciﬁed at all), then the standstill period is deducted from the total time elapsed, so
time runs before and after the period speciﬁed.¹³

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.33(10)
The 3-year period under R.4.33 ran until the application for dismissal under the Rule, was
ﬁled. From May 13 2020, one could not directly apply in a family matter until one got permission
from Family Docket Court. That was done here, and the Notice to Attend Family Docket Court said
that a motion under R.4.33 was desired, though it did not give details. That was the “ﬁling” date
applicable to stop time running.¹
Delay after that (in court processing of the motion) is the court’s,
not any party’s.²
6. Ambush
If the plaintiff is trying hard to get the defendant to take a vital step, and the defendant keeps
stalling and making promises and getting time extensions from the plaintiff, and claiming temporary
inability to perform, the plaintiff can reasonably rely on the defendant’s counsel’s undertakings,
and if the 3 years expires during this, the defendant cannot ambush the plaintiff with that expiry.³
Whether estoppel by conduct would bar a defendant from relying upon R.4.33 is not clear.⁴
And one must distinguish a defendant who does little, from a defendant who fails to perform
its own procedural duties by not cooperating.⁵
7. End of Agreement
A clear rejection by one party of any agreement ends what otherwise could have been one.⁶
When a moratorium by agreement ends, the time out so granted is not automatically added onto
the end, if there is then time to take the necessary step.⁷
8. Defendant’s Participation
If the defendant is uncooperative, can the plaintiff after a time give up and not seek court
help?⁸
No; the defendant never has to assume carriage of the suit, so long as he or she does not
obstruct, stall or delay the suit.⁹
The Rule has an exception in subrule (2) (b)¹⁰
for actual participation
in a proceeding¹¹
by the defendant after the 3 years expire, which codiﬁes
Trout Lake Store v. C. I. B. C.¹²
That exception is triggered by a detailed

R.4.33(10) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
agreed litigation plan ﬁled with the court, even though various mishaps prevented achieving its
deadlines.¹
The defendant need not state that it does not consent to delay, and that or its absence,
is irrelevant.²
Acquiescence by the defendant in the suit’s continuing despite the delay requires
relevant words or acts, not silence.³
Silence after a proposal means silence to a suggested delay,
not to a suggested litigation plan.⁴
Nothing after 3 years’ expiry suggested participation by the
defendant to continue the suit in one case.⁵
Even if ﬁling an application to suspend the suit could
be a participating act, seeking a date when both parties would be available is not acquiescence.⁶
Filing an application to dismiss the action under R.4.33, and if the court denies that, then
in the alternative to dismiss the suit summarily under R.7.3, does not trigger the exception in
subrule (2) (b) ; it calls for an end to the suit, not its continuation, and requiring that the two forms
of dismissal be sought seriatim would just waste time.⁷
The exception in R.4.33(2) (b) occurs where the delay the person seeking to have the action
dismissed has participated in the proceedings to a purpose and extent that the court thinks
warrants the action’s continuing. That exception requires assessing that it is relevant whether the
non-delaying party is slow in moving to dismiss, but that is not fatal if nothing happened during
the interval.⁸
The defendant’s reiteration of its opposition to readiness for trial of the suit, removes any
suggestion that the defendant was participating in the delay.⁹
It is complex how to handle R.4.33 when the suit is now under case management by a judge.¹⁰

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.34(1)
The discretion to preserve the counterclaim or cross action could be exercised only in
appropriate circumstances.¹
The counterclaim was dismissed also where it would be unfair
to eliminate only half the suit and nothing had been usefully done.²
On a motion to dismiss a

should be dismissed.⁴
A King’s Bench Justice did not know of any Alberta case where the defendant’s counterclaim
was allowed to proceed, but the main claim was dismissed for want of prosecution. Moving one of
the two forward moves forward the whole suit?⁵
DIVISION 7
TRANSFER AND TRANSMISSION OF INTEREST
Stay of proceedings on transfer or transmission of interest
4.34(1) If at any time in an action prior to judgment the interest or liability of a
party is transferred or transmitted to another person by assignment, bankruptcy,
death or other means, the action is stayed until an order to continue the action
by or against the other person has been obtained.
Related Provisions
12.35 (no stay of divorce action).

(2) If a transfer or transmission of the interest or liability of a party takes place
while an application or proceeding in an action is pending, an interested person
may, on ﬁling an afﬁdavit verifying the transfer or transmission of the interest or
liability and without notice to any other party, request the Court to order that the
action continue.⁶
(3) An order to continue the action must be served on each of the other parties
as soon as it is received by the party requesting the order.
(4) If an order to continue an action is not made within a reasonable time after
the date on which the action is stayed, the defendant or respondent may apply
to the Court to have the action dismissed for delay under rule 4.31 [Application
to deal with delay].⁷

4-122
R.4.34(4) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
See R.3.74 nn. G, H.
If a plaintiff assigns its interest in the suit, R.4.34 automatically stays the suit. Then either the
plaintiff has to move under R.4.34 to continue the suit, or the defendant has to move to dismiss
the action under R.4.31. If there is an issue about continuing, the reason for the assignment is
relevant, apparently to detect champerty. Under 8 months between the assignment was not undue
delay.¹
If one party to a pending suit dies that may stay the action as against him, but it does not stay
the whole action against everyone.²
The continuation has to be against all or none of the defendants.³
This Rule does not bar all motions by a defendant except one to substitute the dead plaintiff’s
personal representative, reading it in context of the other Rules.⁴
Many civil and criminal proceedings name the Sovereign as a party, which then required an
amendment to the style of cause when the Queen died. The Court of Appeal has issued a blanket
order and a Notice to the Profession and Public greatly simplifying that change in Court of Appeal
proceedings.⁵
There is a review of the law on champerty and maintenance, and exceptions to the general
rule, such as “ancillary to a property interest” and “a legitimate commercial interest”.⁶
Also of what
is a proper or improper motive for maintenance (which in turn is necessary for champerty).⁷
The section of the Business Corporations Act protecting “rights acquired by any person prior
to the revival” on the revival of a company to the register does not refer to a potential defence of
lack of parties.⁸
Rule 12.35 keeps a divorce action going despite bankruptcy, but R.4.34 stays a matrimonial
property action until the court orders it continued. There is ordinarily no reason to stall such a suit,
especially where full disclosure is needed.⁹
Rule 4.34 does not bar an application under R.4.33, nor does the stay period created by R.
4.34 count under a R.4.33 application.¹⁰
The effect of R.4.34’s stay on the 3-year “drop-dead” R.4.33, is discussed, suggesting that R.
4.33 is interrupted by R.4.34 proceedings to get an order to continue the suit.¹¹

4-123
PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.35
Death has No Effect on Action After Evidence Heard
4.35 If a party dies before judgment but after all the evidence has been heard,
(a) the death does not terminate the action, whether or not the claim
survives death, and
(b) judgment may be pronounced and entered despite the death.¹

This is a necessary Rule, now that some causes of action and heads of damage no longer
survive death. Sick people trying to rush to trial have died just before judgment. At least under this
Rule, the judge’s taking time to draft a reserved judgment does not retroactively throw the estate
out of court. But the defendant’s temptation to stall survives.
DIVISION 8
DISCONTINUANCE
Discontinuance of Claim
4.36(1) Before a date is set for trial, a plaintiff may discontinue all or any part of
an action against one or more defendants.²
(2) After a trial date has been set but before a trial starts, a plaintiff may
discontinue all or part of an action against one or more defendants only
(a) with the written agreement of every party, or
(b) with the Court’s permission.
(3) After the trial starts, a plaintiff may discontinue all or part of an action only
with the Court’s permission.
(4) A discontinuance under this rule must be in Form 23 and must be ﬁled
and served on each of the other parties and, after the plaintiff serves notice of
discontinuance, the defendant is entitled to a costs award against the plaintiff
for having defended against the discontinued claim.³
(5) The discontinuance of the action may not be raised as a defence to any
subsequent action for the same or substantially the same claim.⁴

Related Provisions
10.35 (assessment of costs) ; 14.66 (discontinuance of appeal)

4-124
R.4.36(5) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
A. References
Generally on discontinuance, see the textbooks.¹
B. Permission to Discontinue
Sometimes leave to discontinue is needed, and subrules (2) and (3) are not the only instances.²
There is no signiﬁcant difference between the 1968 and 2010 Rules on the subject of when
court permission to discontinue an action is necessary.³
There is considerable doubt on the various
rationales usually advanced for needing leave to discontinue. If continuing the action at least in
part would not be futile, a request that the plaintiff be immunized from costs when discontinuing
should be denied.⁴
Where the parties still are married, matrimonial property proceedings often do not give the
plaintiff an unqualiﬁed right to discontinue the suit, as the suit is not merely for beneﬁts from the
defendant to the plaintiff (but also vice versa). And outstanding other issues in the lawsuit may bar
a discontinuance. That is doubly so where the defendant ﬁled a statement of defence (not a mere
demand for notice) and disputed certain proposals in the statement of claim.⁵
The general rule is that a plaintiff (or the equivalent) may withdraw (“discontinue”) the suit
unilaterally without permission⁶
without creating res judicata or other bar to a new suit for the
same thing. But the plaintiff will ordinarily have to pay all the costs of the suit so dropped.⁷
The
court will sometimes impose other terms and conditions, even a bar on future similar suits.⁸
The
new Rule makes it clear that a plaintiff can also drop one claim from the suit and leave the rest.⁹
C. Effect of Discontinuance
If a suit is discontinued, that would end a pending appeal in it, even if it is a constitutional issue
and there are interveners.¹⁰
But the court will bar an abusive discontinuance, e. g. to thwart an
outstanding summary dismissal suit and then re-sue elsewhere.¹¹
A defendant held liable by a summary judgment application can pursue claims against co-
defendants even if the main suit is discontinued? But can the defendant pursue its claim against

PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION R.4.36(5)
co-defendants by notice after discontinuance of the main suit? The court was not sure, so it used
R.1.4 to order that the plaintiff not discontinue against the other defendants (without leave).¹
If an action is discontinued, any motions in it that were pending also come to an end. The
implied undertaking survives the end of the litigation. The implied undertaking is only extinguished
if the information is used in open court.²
D. Agreement
If it has been agreed that one party will drop a certain claim, it is not necessary to get an
amendment; that party can just discontinue.³
After a Pierringer (partial) settlement contract, a
plaintiff has the right to discontinue against any defendant if the plaintiff wishes.⁴
E. Inactive Suits
A plaintiff cannot use the delay Rules to have her own suit dismissed. (It was attempted here
to avoid costs usually payable on discontinuance. )⁵
On an attempt to discontinue a matrimonial
property suit inactive after the defendant was noted in default, to claim survivorship after the
defendant’s recent death, see the Coyle case.⁶
One cannot discontinue after a divorce decree.⁷
A plaintiff was not allowed to discontinue a limping matrimonial property suit, as there was
no statement of defence ﬁled, thereby admitting liability and the relief claimed, so both sides
admitted that property distribution was needed, and the statement of claim unusually claimed
relief beneﬁcial to both sides; and a substantial fund was held in court.⁸
F. Reversing Discontinuance
The court may also allow a plaintiff to withdraw a discontinuance and so reinstate the suit which
it had dropped, but here imposition of terms and conditions is quite likely, to undo any prejudice
created.⁹
And if the limitation period has expired, that may well bar reinstatement.¹⁰
Discontinuance
close to trial prejudices the many other litigants who have been denied a trial date by setting this
case down for that date.¹¹
The defendant cannot object to the discontinuance in order to try to get
a dismissal instead, to use in other countries, especially if they are better forums.¹²

4-126
R.4.37(1) PART 4: MANAGING LITIGATION
G. Miscellaneous
A discontinuance is no admission that commencing the suit, or mode of commencement, was
wrong.¹
A suit was discontinued, which was not an abuse of process, nor was there any application
to set aside the discontinuance. So the merits should not be ruled on, and only costs should be
ruled on. The defendant had ﬁled evidence conceding the plaintiff’s right to all relief except costs.²
Abandoning an originating application about to be argued is very similar to discontinuing an
action (by statement of claim) after it has been set down for trial; by analogy R.4.36(2) (b) requires
leave. And leave can be given on terms. One term possible is to bar the party from suing again for
the same thing. That condition does not have to be spelled out exactly in the Rule.³
A satisfaction piece may not be the right piece of paper, but a judgment creditor must have the
ability unilaterally to abandon the unpaid portion of the judgment debt payable to that creditor.⁴
A partial discontinuance (against certain defendants) was held to restart time running under
R.4.33.⁵
Where it becomes obvious that an application has become moot, the respondent’s appearance
to argue the merits thereafter may not attract costs.⁶
The Chief Justice of the Court of King's Bench asks that counsel discontinuing or settling a
suit tell the Trial Coordinators.⁷
Discontinuance of Defence
4.37(1) A defendant may discontinue the whole of a statement of defence by
ﬁling a notice of discontinuance in Form 24 and serving it on the plaintiff.
(2) On ﬁling the notice of discontinuance,
(a) the defendant is in default of defence, and
(b) the plaintiff is entitled to a costs award against the defendant for
having responded to the discontinued defence.

This Rule is entirely new in 2010.
